/-
We define a function `uniqueSorted` that takes a list of integers, removes duplicates, and sorts the result. We then prove that this function satisfies the postcondition that the result is a permutation of the input with duplicates removed and is sorted.
-/
import Codetic
import Mathlib
-- set_option trace.codetic true
-- set_option trace.codetic.zzh_custom true

set_option autoImplicit false

@[reducible, simp] def uniqueSorted_precond (arr : List Int) : Prop := True

/-
The postcondition states that the result is a permutation of the input with duplicates removed, and the result is sorted.
-/
@[reducible, simp] def uniqueSorted_postcond (arr : List Int) (result: List Int) (h_precond : uniqueSorted_precond (arr)) : Prop :=
  List.Perm arr.eraseDups result ∧ List.Pairwise (· ≤ ·) result

def uniqueSorted (arr : List Int) (h_precond : uniqueSorted_precond (arr)) : List Int :=
  let rec insert (x : Int) (sorted : List Int) : List Int :=
    match sorted with
    | [] => [x]
    | head :: tail =>
      if x <= head then x :: head :: tail
      else head :: insert x tail
  let rec insertionSort (xs : List Int) : List Int :=
    match xs with
    | [] => []
    | h :: t =>
      let sortedTail := insertionSort t
      insert h sortedTail
  let removeDups := fun (xs : List Int) =>
    let rec aux (remaining : List Int) (seen : List Int) (acc : List Int) : List Int :=
      match remaining with
      | [] => acc.reverse
      | h :: t =>
        if h ∈ seen then aux t seen acc
        else aux t (h :: seen) (h :: acc)
    aux xs [] []
  insertionSort (removeDups arr)

theorem uniqueSorted_spec_satisfied (arr: List Int) (h_precond : uniqueSorted_precond (arr)) :
  uniqueSorted_postcond (arr) (uniqueSorted (arr) h_precond) h_precond := by
    -- By definition of `uniqueSorted`, we know that the result is a permutation of the input with duplicates removed and sorted.

@[reducible, simp] def uniqueSorted_precond (arr : List Int) : Prop := True

/-

@[reducible, simp] def uniqueSorted_postcond (arr : List Int) (result: List Int) (h_precond : uniqueSorted_precond (arr)) : Prop :=

      | [] => acc.reverse

        if h ∈ seen then aux t seen acc
        else aux t (h :: seen) (h :: acc)
    aux xs [] []
  insertionSort (removeDups arr)

theorem uniqueSorted_spec_satisfied (arr: List Int) (h_precond : uniqueSorted_precond (arr)) :
  uniqueSorted_postcond (arr) (uniqueSorted (arr) h_precond) h_precond := by
    -- By definition of `uniqueSorted`, we know that the result is a permutation of the input with duplicates removed and sorted.
    have h_perm : List.Perm (List.eraseDups arr) (uniqueSorted arr h_precond) := by
      -- By definition of `insert`, we know that inserting an element into a list preserves the permutation property.
      have h_insert_perm : ∀ (x : ℤ) (l : List ℤ), List.Perm (x :: l) (uniqueSorted.insert x l) := by
        -- We can prove this by induction on the list `l`.
        --codetic?可以证明
        sorry
      -- By definition of `insertionSort`, we know that inserting elements into a list preserves the permutation property.
      have h_insertionSort_perm : ∀ (l : List ℤ), List.Perm l (uniqueSorted.insertionSort l) := by
        --codetic?无法证明
        intro l; induction' l with x l ih; aesop;
        --codetic?无法证明
        convert List.Perm.trans ( List.Perm.cons _ ih ) ( h_insert_perm _ _ ) using 1;
      -- By definition of `uniqueSorted`, we know that `uniqueSorted arr h_precond` is the insertion sort of the list obtained by removing duplicates from `arr`.
      simp [uniqueSorted];
      -- By definition of `uniqueSorted.aux`, we know that `uniqueSorted.aux arr [] []` is the list obtained by removing duplicates from `arr`.
      have h_aux_eq_eraseDups : ∀ (l : List ℤ) (seen : List ℤ) (acc : List ℤ), uniqueSorted.aux l seen acc = acc.reverse ++ (l.filter (fun x => x ∉ seen)).eraseDups := by

    have h_perm : List.Perm (List.eraseDups arr) (uniqueSorted arr h_precond) := by

      have h_insert_perm : ∀ (x : ℤ) (l : List ℤ), List.Perm (x :: l) (uniqueSorted.insert x l) := by

        --codetic?可以证明

/-
We define the `twoSum` function and its specification.
We introduce a helper function `twoSum_aux` that mirrors the internal loop of `twoSum` but is easier to reason about.
We prove that `twoSum` is equivalent to `twoSum_aux` with appropriate initial arguments.
We prove the soundness of `twoSum_aux`: if it returns a result, that result is a valid solution.
We prove the completeness of `twoSum_aux`: if a unique solution exists, `twoSum_aux` will find it given sufficient fuel.
Finally, we prove `twoSum_spec_satisfied`, showing that `twoSum` satisfies the postcondition given the precondition.
-/

import Mathlib

set_option linter.mathlibStandardSet false

open scoped BigOperators
open scoped Real
open scoped Nat
open scoped Classical
open scoped Pointwise

set_option maxHeartbeats 0
set_option maxRecDepth 4000
set_option synthInstance.maxHeartbeats 20000
set_option synthInstance.maxSize 128

set_option relaxedAutoImplicit false
set_option autoImplicit false

noncomputable section

@[reducible] def twoSum_precond (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) : Prop :=
  -- The array must have at least 2 elements
  nums.size ≥ 2 ∧
  -- There exists exactly one pair of indices whose values sum to the target
  (List.range nums.size).any (fun i =>
    (List.range i).any (fun j => nums[i]! + nums[j]! = target)) ∧
  -- No other pair sums to the target (ensuring uniqueness of solution)
  ((List.range nums.size).flatMap (fun i =>
    (List.range i).filter (fun j => nums[i]! + nums[j]! = target))).length = 1

def twoSum (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (h_precond : twoSum_precond (nums) (target)) : Array Nat :=
  let rec findIndices (i : Nat) (j : Nat) (fuel : Nat) : Array Nat :=
    match fuel with
    | 0 => #[] -- Fuel exhausted, return empty array
    | fuel+1 =>
      if i >= nums.size then
        #[] -- No solution found
      else if j >= nums.size then
        findIndices (i + 1) (i + 2) fuel -- Move to next i and reset j
      else
        if nums[i]! + nums[j]! == target then
          #[i, j] -- Found solution
        else
          findIndices i (j + 1) fuel -- Try next j
   findIndices 0 1 (nums.size * nums.size)

@[reducible] def twoSum_postcond (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (result: Array Nat) (h_precond : twoSum_precond (nums) (target)) : Prop :=
  -- Result contains exactly 2 indices
  result.size = 2 ∧
  -- The indices are valid (within bounds of the nums array)
  result[0]! < nums.size ∧ result[1]! < nums.size ∧
  -- The indices are in ascending order (sorted)
  result[0]! < result[1]! ∧
  -- The values at these indices sum to the target
  nums[result[0]!]! + nums[result[1]!]! = target

/-
A helper function mirroring the logic of `twoSum`'s internal loop.
-/
def twoSum_aux (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (i j fuel : Nat) : Array Nat :=
  match fuel with
  | 0 => #[]
  | fuel+1 =>
    if i >= nums.size then
      #[]
    else if j >= nums.size then
      twoSum_aux nums target (i + 1) (i + 2) fuel
    else
      if nums[i]! + nums[j]! == target then
        #[i, j]
      else
        twoSum_aux nums target i (j + 1) fuel

theorem twoSum_eq_aux (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (h : twoSum_precond nums target) :
  twoSum nums target h = twoSum_aux nums target 0 1 (nums.size * nums.size) := by
  rw [twoSum]
  let rec findIndices_eq_aux (i j fuel : Nat) :
    twoSum.findIndices nums target i j fuel = twoSum_aux nums target i j fuel := by

  ((List.range nums.size).flatMap (fun i =>
    (List.range i).filter (fun j => nums[i]! + nums[j]! = target))).length = 1

def twoSum (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (h_precond : twoSum_precond (nums) (target)) : Array Nat :=
  let rec findIndices (i : Nat) (j : Nat) (fuel : Nat) : Array Nat :=
    match fuel with
    | 0 => #[] -- Fuel exhausted, return empty array
    | fuel+1 =>
      if i >= nums.size then
        #[] -- No solution found
      else if j >= nums.size then
        findIndices (i + 1) (i + 2) fuel -- Move to next i and reset j
      else
        if nums[i]! + nums[j]! == target then
          #[i, j] -- Found solution
        else
          findIndices i (j + 1) fuel -- Try next j
   findIndices 0 1 (nums.size * nums.size)

@[reducible] def twoSum_postcond (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (result: Array Nat) (h_precond : twoSum_precond (nums) (target)) : Prop :=
  -- Result contains exactly 2 indices
  result.size = 2 ∧
  -- The indices are valid (within bounds of the nums array)
  result[0]! < nums.size ∧ result[1]! < nums.size ∧
  -- The indices are in ascending order (sorted)
  result[0]! < result[1]! ∧
  -- The values at these indices sum to the target
  nums[result[0]!]! + nums[result[1]!]! = target

/-
A helper function mirroring the logic of `twoSum`'s internal loop.
-/
def twoSum_aux (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (i j fuel : Nat) : Array Nat :=
  match fuel with
  | 0 => #[]
  | fuel+1 =>
    if i >= nums.size then
      #[]
    else if j >= nums.size then
      twoSum_aux nums target (i + 1) (i + 2) fuel
    else
      if nums[i]! + nums[j]! == target then
        #[i, j]
      else
        twoSum_aux nums target i (j + 1) fuel

theorem twoSum_eq_aux (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (h : twoSum_precond nums target) :
  twoSum nums target h = twoSum_aux nums target 0 1 (nums.size * nums.size) := by
  rw [twoSum]
  let rec findIndices_eq_aux (i j fuel : Nat) :
    twoSum.findIndices nums target i j fuel = twoSum_aux nums target i j fuel := by
    induction fuel generalizing i j
    case zero => rfl
    case succ fuel ih =>
      rw [twoSum.findIndices, twoSum_aux]
      split
      · rfl
      · split
        · rw [ih]
        · split
          · rfl
          · rw [ih]
  apply findIndices_eq_aux

If `twoSum_aux` returns a result of size 2, then the result contains valid indices `u, v` such that `u < v` and `nums[u] + nums[v] = target`. This assumes the invariant `i < j` holds for the inputs.
-/
theorem twoSum_aux_soundness (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (i j fuel : Nat)
  (h_ij : i < j) :
  let res := twoSum_aux nums target i j fuel
  res.size = 2 →
  res[0]! < nums.size ∧ res[1]! < nums.size ∧ res[0]! < res[1]! ∧ nums[res[0]!]! + nums[res[1]!]! = target := by

  (hu : u < nums.size) (hv : v < nums.size) (huv : u < v)
  (h_target : nums[u]! + nums[v]! = target)
  (h_unique_row : ∀ k, u < k → k < v → nums[u]! + nums[k]! ≠ target)
  (j : Nat) (hj_le_v : j ≤ v) (hj_gt_u : u < j)
  (h_fuel : fuel > v - j) :
  twoSum_aux nums target u j fuel = #[u, v] := by

    rcases fuel with ( _ | fuel ) <;> simp_all +decide [ twoSum_aux ];
    grind

/-
If row `i` contains no solution for indices `k >= j`, then `twoSum_aux` will eventually skip row `i` and proceed to row `i+1`, consuming the appropriate amount of fuel.
-/
theorem twoSum_aux_skip_row (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (i j fuel : Nat)
  (h_no_sol : ∀ k, j ≤ k → k < nums.size → nums[i]! + nums[k]! ≠ target)
  (h_j_le_size : j ≤ nums.size)
  (h_fuel : fuel ≥ nums.size - j + 1) :
  twoSum_aux nums target i j fuel = twoSum_aux nums target (i + 1) (i + 2) (fuel - (nums.size - j + 1)) := by
    induction' h : nums.size - j with k hk generalizing i j fuel;
    · cases h_j_le_size.eq_or_lt <;> simp_all +decide [ Nat.sub_eq_iff_eq_add ];
      rcases fuel with ( _ | _ | fuel ) <;> simp_all +decide [ twoSum_aux ];
      intros; linarith;
    · convert hk i ( j + 1 ) ( fuel - 1 ) ( fun k hk₁ hk₂ => h_no_sol k ( by linarith ) hk₂ ) ( by omega ) ( by omega ) _ using 1;
      · rcases fuel with ( _ | fuel ) <;> simp_all +decide;
        rw [ twoSum_aux ];
        split_ifs <;> simp_all +decide [ Nat.sub_add_cancel ];
        exact Nat.recOn fuel ( by rfl ) fun n ihn => by rw [ twoSum_aux ] ; aesop;
      · rw [ Nat.sub_sub ];
        ring;
      · omega

/-
The precondition guarantees the existence of a unique pair of indices `(u, v)` such that `u < v` and their values sum to `target`.
-/
theorem exists_unique_pair (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (h : twoSum_precond nums target) :
  ∃ u v, u < v ∧ v < nums.size ∧ nums[u]! + nums[v]! = target ∧
  ∀ a b, a < b → b < nums.size → nums[a]! + nums[b]! = target → a = u ∧ b = v := by
  rcases h with ⟨ h₁, h₂, h₃ ⟩;
  have h_unique : ∃ p : ℕ × ℕ, p ∈ (List.range nums.size).flatMap (fun i => (List.range i).filter (fun j => nums[i]! + nums[j]! = target) |>.map (fun j => (i, j))) ∧ ∀ q : ℕ × ℕ, q ∈ (List.range nums.size).flatMap (fun i => (List.range i).filter (fun j => nums[i]! + nums[j]! = target) |>.map (fun j => (i, j))) → q = p := by

/-
We define the `twoSum` function and its specification.
We introduce a helper function `twoSum_aux` that mirrors the internal loop of `twoSum` but is easier to reason about.
We prove that `twoSum` is equivalent to `twoSum_aux` with appropriate initial arguments.
We prove the soundness of `twoSum_aux`: if it returns a result, that result is a valid solution.
We prove the completeness of `twoSum_aux`: if a unique solution exists, `twoSum_aux` will find it given sufficient fuel.
Finally, we prove `twoSum_spec_satisfied`, showing that `twoSum` satisfies the postcondition given the precondition.
-/

import Mathlib

set_option linter.mathlibStandardSet false

open scoped BigOperators
open scoped Real
open scoped Nat
open scoped Classical
open scoped Pointwise

set_option maxHeartbeats 0
set_option maxRecDepth 4000
set_option synthInstance.maxHeartbeats 20000
set_option synthInstance.maxSize 128

set_option relaxedAutoImplicit false
set_option autoImplicit false

noncomputable section

@[reducible] def twoSum_precond (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) : Prop :=
  -- The array must have at least 2 elements
  nums.size ≥ 2 ∧
  -- There exists exactly one pair of indices whose values sum to the target
  (List.range nums.size).any (fun i =>
    (List.range i).any (fun j => nums[i]! + nums[j]! = target)) ∧
  -- No other pair sums to the target (ensuring uniqueness of solution)
  ((List.range nums.size).flatMap (fun i =>
    (List.range i).filter (fun j => nums[i]! + nums[j]! = target))).length = 1

def twoSum (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (h_precond : twoSum_precond (nums) (target)) : Array Nat :=
  let rec findIndices (i : Nat) (j : Nat) (fuel : Nat) : Array Nat :=
    match fuel with
    | 0 => #[] -- Fuel exhausted, return empty array
    | fuel+1 =>
      if i >= nums.size then
        #[] -- No solution found
      else if j >= nums.size then
        findIndices (i + 1) (i + 2) fuel -- Move to next i and reset j
      else
        if nums[i]! + nums[j]! == target then
          #[i, j] -- Found solution
        else
          findIndices i (j + 1) fuel -- Try next j
   findIndices 0 1 (nums.size * nums.size)

@[reducible] def twoSum_postcond (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (result: Array Nat) (h_precond : twoSum_precond (nums) (target)) : Prop :=
  -- Result contains exactly 2 indices
  result.size = 2 ∧
  -- The indices are valid (within bounds of the nums array)
  result[0]! < nums.size ∧ result[1]! < nums.size ∧
  -- The indices are in ascending order (sorted)
  result[0]! < result[1]! ∧
  -- The values at these indices sum to the target
  nums[result[0]!]! + nums[result[1]!]! = target

/-
A helper function mirroring the logic of `twoSum`'s internal loop.
-/
def twoSum_aux (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (i j fuel : Nat) : Array Nat :=
  match fuel with
  | 0 => #[]
  | fuel+1 =>
    if i >= nums.size then
      #[]
    else if j >= nums.size then
      twoSum_aux nums target (i + 1) (i + 2) fuel
    else
      if nums[i]! + nums[j]! == target then
        #[i, j]
      else
        twoSum_aux nums target i (j + 1) fuel

theorem twoSum_eq_aux (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (h : twoSum_precond nums target) :
  twoSum nums target h = twoSum_aux nums target 0 1 (nums.size * nums.size) := by
  rw [twoSum]
  let rec findIndices_eq_aux (i j fuel : Nat) :
    twoSum.findIndices nums target i j fuel = twoSum_aux nums target i j fuel := by
    induction fuel generalizing i j
    case zero => rfl
    case succ fuel ih =>
      rw [twoSum.findIndices, twoSum_aux]
      split
      · rfl
      · split
        · rw [ih]
        · split
          · rfl
          · rw [ih]
  apply findIndices_eq_aux

/-
If `twoSum_aux` returns a result of size 2, then the result contains valid indices `u, v` such that `u < v` and `nums[u] + nums[v] = target`. This assumes the invariant `i < j` holds for the inputs.
-/
theorem twoSum_aux_soundness (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (i j fuel : Nat)
  (h_ij : i < j) :
  let res := twoSum_aux nums target i j fuel
  res.size = 2 →
  res[0]! < nums.size ∧ res[1]! < nums.size ∧ res[0]! < res[1]! ∧ nums[res[0]!]! + nums[res[1]!]! = target := by
    induction' fuel with fuel ih generalizing i j;
    · cases i <;> cases j <;> tauto;
    · simp +zetaDelta at * ; by_cases hj : j ≥ nums.size <;> simp_all +decide [ twoSum_aux ];
      · grind;
      · grind

/-
If `twoSum_aux` is searching in the row `u` where the solution `(u, v)` is located, and `j <= v`, and no other solution exists in this row before `v`, then it returns `#[u, v]`.
-/
theorem twoSum_aux_in_row (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (u v : Nat) (fuel : Nat)
  (hu : u < nums.size) (hv : v < nums.size) (huv : u < v)
  (h_target : nums[u]! + nums[v]! = target)
  (h_unique_row : ∀ k, u < k → k < v → nums[u]! + nums[k]! ≠ target)
  (j : Nat) (hj_le_v : j ≤ v) (hj_gt_u : u < j)
  (h_fuel : fuel > v - j) :
  twoSum_aux nums target u j fuel = #[u, v] := by
    induction' n : v - j using Nat.strong_induction_on with n ih generalizing j fuel;
    rcases fuel with ( _ | fuel ) <;> simp_all +decide [ twoSum_aux ];
    grind

/-
If row `i` contains no solution for indices `k >= j`, then `twoSum_aux` will eventually skip row `i` and proceed to row `i+1`, consuming the appropriate amount of fuel.
-/
theorem twoSum_aux_skip_row (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (i j fuel : Nat)
  (h_no_sol : ∀ k, j ≤ k → k < nums.size → nums[i]! + nums[k]! ≠ target)
  (h_j_le_size : j ≤ nums.size)
  (h_fuel : fuel ≥ nums.size - j + 1) :
  twoSum_aux nums target i j fuel = twoSum_aux nums target (i + 1) (i + 2) (fuel - (nums.size - j + 1)) := by
    induction' h : nums.size - j with k hk generalizing i j fuel;
    · cases h_j_le_size.eq_or_lt <;> simp_all +decide [ Nat.sub_eq_iff_eq_add ];
      rcases fuel with ( _ | _ | fuel ) <;> simp_all +decide [ twoSum_aux ];
      intros; linarith;
    · convert hk i ( j + 1 ) ( fuel - 1 ) ( fun k hk₁ hk₂ => h_no_sol k ( by linarith ) hk₂ ) ( by omega ) ( by omega ) _ using 1;
      · rcases fuel with ( _ | fuel ) <;> simp_all +decide;
        rw [ twoSum_aux ];
        split_ifs <;> simp_all +decide [ Nat.sub_add_cancel ];
        exact Nat.recOn fuel ( by rfl ) fun n ihn => by rw [ twoSum_aux ] ; aesop;
      · rw [ Nat.sub_sub ];
        ring;
      · omega

/-
The precondition guarantees the existence of a unique pair of indices `(u, v)` such that `u < v` and their values sum to `target`.
-/
theorem exists_unique_pair (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (h : twoSum_precond nums target) :
  ∃ u v, u < v ∧ v < nums.size ∧ nums[u]! + nums[v]! = target ∧
  ∀ a b, a < b → b < nums.size → nums[a]! + nums[b]! = target → a = u ∧ b = v := by
  rcases h with ⟨ h₁, h₂, h₃ ⟩;
  have h_unique : ∃ p : ℕ × ℕ, p ∈ (List.range nums.size).flatMap (fun i => (List.range i).filter (fun j => nums[i]! + nums[j]! = target) |>.map (fun j => (i, j))) ∧ ∀ q : ℕ × ℕ, q ∈ (List.range nums.size).flatMap (fun i => (List.range i).filter (fun j => nums[i]! + nums[j]! = target) |>.map (fun j => (i, j))) → q = p := by
    have h_unique : List.length (List.flatMap (fun i => (List.range i).filter (fun j => nums[i]! + nums[j]! = target) |>.map (fun j => (i, j))) (List.range nums.size)) = 1 := by
      convert h₃ using 1;
      induction ( List.range nums.size ) <;> aesop;
    rw [ List.length_eq_one_iff ] at h_unique; obtain ⟨ p, hp ⟩ := h_unique; use p; aesop;
  obtain ⟨ ⟨ u, v ⟩, hp₁, hp₂ ⟩ := h_unique;
  simp +zetaDelta at *;
  exact ⟨ v, u, hp₁.2.1, hp₁.1, by linarith, fun a b hab hba h => by specialize hp₂ _ _ b hba a hab ( by linarith ) rfl rfl; aesop ⟩

/-
If `(u, v)` is the unique solution with `u < v`, and we start searching at row `i \le u` with sufficient fuel, `twoSum_aux` will return `#[u, v]`.
-/
theorem twoSum_aux_finds_unique (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (u v : Nat)
  (hu : u < nums.size) (hv : v < nums.size) (huv : u < v)
  (h_target : nums[u]! + nums[v]! = target)
  (h_unique : ∀ a b, a < b → b < nums.size → nums[a]! + nums[b]! = target → a = u ∧ b = v)
  (i : Nat) (hi : i ≤ u)
  (fuel : Nat) (h_fuel : fuel ≥ (u - i + 1) * nums.size) :
  twoSum_aux nums target i (i + 1) fuel = #[u, v] := by
    induction' h : u - i with d hd generalizing i fuel;
    · -- Apply the `twoSum_aux_in_row` theorem to show that the result is `#[u, v]`.
      have h_aux : twoSum_aux nums target i (i + 1) fuel = #[i, v] := by
        have h_no_sol : ∀ k, i < k → k < v → nums[i]! + nums[k]! ≠ target := by

  let rec findIndices (i : Nat) (j : Nat) (fuel : Nat) : Array Nat :=
    match fuel with
    | 0 => #[] -- Fuel exhausted, return empty array
    | fuel+1 =>
      if i >= nums.size then
        #[] -- No solution found
      else if j >= nums.size then
        findIndices (i + 1) (i + 2) fuel -- Move to next i and reset j
      else
        if nums[i]! + nums[j]! == target then
          #[i, j] -- Found solution
        else
          findIndices i (j + 1) fuel -- Try next j
   findIndices 0 1 (nums.size * nums.size)

@[reducible] def twoSum_postcond (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (result: Array Nat) (h_precond : twoSum_precond (nums) (target)) : Prop :=
  -- Result contains exactly 2 indices
  result.size = 2 ∧
  -- The indices are valid (within bounds of the nums array)
  result[0]! < nums.size ∧ result[1]! < nums.size ∧
  -- The indices are in ascending order (sorted)
  result[0]! < result[1]! ∧
  -- The values at these indices sum to the target
  nums[result[0]!]! + nums[result[1]!]! = target

/-
A helper function mirroring the logic of `twoSum`'s internal loop.
-/
def twoSum_aux (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (i j fuel : Nat) : Array Nat :=
  match fuel with
  | 0 => #[]
  | fuel+1 =>
    if i >= nums.size then
      #[]
    else if j >= nums.size then
      twoSum_aux nums target (i + 1) (i + 2) fuel
    else
      if nums[i]! + nums[j]! == target then
        #[i, j]
      else
        twoSum_aux nums target i (j + 1) fuel

theorem twoSum_eq_aux (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (h : twoSum_precond nums target) :
  twoSum nums target h = twoSum_aux nums target 0 1 (nums.size * nums.size) := by
  rw [twoSum]
  let rec findIndices_eq_aux (i j fuel : Nat) :
    twoSum.findIndices nums target i j fuel = twoSum_aux nums target i j fuel := by
    induction fuel generalizing i j
    case zero => rfl
    case succ fuel ih =>
      rw [twoSum.findIndices, twoSum_aux]
      split
      · rfl
      · split
        · rw [ih]
        · split
          · rfl
          · rw [ih]
  apply findIndices_eq_aux

/-
If `twoSum_aux` returns a result of size 2, then the result contains valid indices `u, v` such that `u < v` and `nums[u] + nums[v] = target`. This assumes the invariant `i < j` holds for the inputs.
-/
theorem twoSum_aux_soundness (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (i j fuel : Nat)
  (h_ij : i < j) :
  let res := twoSum_aux nums target i j fuel
  res.size = 2 →
  res[0]! < nums.size ∧ res[1]! < nums.size ∧ res[0]! < res[1]! ∧ nums[res[0]!]! + nums[res[1]!]! = target := by
    induction' fuel with fuel ih generalizing i j;
    · cases i <;> cases j <;> tauto;
    · simp +zetaDelta at * ; by_cases hj : j ≥ nums.size <;> simp_all +decide [ twoSum_aux ];
      · grind;
      · grind

/-
If `twoSum_aux` is searching in the row `u` where the solution `(u, v)` is located, and `j <= v`, and no other solution exists in this row before `v`, then it returns `#[u, v]`.
-/
theorem twoSum_aux_in_row (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (u v : Nat) (fuel : Nat)
  (hu : u < nums.size) (hv : v < nums.size) (huv : u < v)
  (h_target : nums[u]! + nums[v]! = target)
  (h_unique_row : ∀ k, u < k → k < v → nums[u]! + nums[k]! ≠ target)
  (j : Nat) (hj_le_v : j ≤ v) (hj_gt_u : u < j)
  (h_fuel : fuel > v - j) :
  twoSum_aux nums target u j fuel = #[u, v] := by
    induction' n : v - j using Nat.strong_induction_on with n ih generalizing j fuel;
    rcases fuel with ( _ | fuel ) <;> simp_all +decide [ twoSum_aux ];
    grind

/-
If row `i` contains no solution for indices `k >= j`, then `twoSum_aux` will eventually skip row `i` and proceed to row `i+1`, consuming the appropriate amount of fuel.
-/
theorem twoSum_aux_skip_row (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (i j fuel : Nat)
  (h_no_sol : ∀ k, j ≤ k → k < nums.size → nums[i]! + nums[k]! ≠ target)
  (h_j_le_size : j ≤ nums.size)
  (h_fuel : fuel ≥ nums.size - j + 1) :
  twoSum_aux nums target i j fuel = twoSum_aux nums target (i + 1) (i + 2) (fuel - (nums.size - j + 1)) := by
    induction' h : nums.size - j with k hk generalizing i j fuel;
    · cases h_j_le_size.eq_or_lt <;> simp_all +decide [ Nat.sub_eq_iff_eq_add ];
      rcases fuel with ( _ | _ | fuel ) <;> simp_all +decide [ twoSum_aux ];
      intros; linarith;
    · convert hk i ( j + 1 ) ( fuel - 1 ) ( fun k hk₁ hk₂ => h_no_sol k ( by linarith ) hk₂ ) ( by omega ) ( by omega ) _ using 1;
      · rcases fuel with ( _ | fuel ) <;> simp_all +decide;
        rw [ twoSum_aux ];
        split_ifs <;> simp_all +decide [ Nat.sub_add_cancel ];
        exact Nat.recOn fuel ( by rfl ) fun n ihn => by rw [ twoSum_aux ] ; aesop;
      · rw [ Nat.sub_sub ];
        ring;
      · omega

/-
The precondition guarantees the existence of a unique pair of indices `(u, v)` such that `u < v` and their values sum to `target`.
-/
theorem exists_unique_pair (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (h : twoSum_precond nums target) :
  ∃ u v, u < v ∧ v < nums.size ∧ nums[u]! + nums[v]! = target ∧
  ∀ a b, a < b → b < nums.size → nums[a]! + nums[b]! = target → a = u ∧ b = v := by
  rcases h with ⟨ h₁, h₂, h₃ ⟩;
  have h_unique : ∃ p : ℕ × ℕ, p ∈ (List.range nums.size).flatMap (fun i => (List.range i).filter (fun j => nums[i]! + nums[j]! = target) |>.map (fun j => (i, j))) ∧ ∀ q : ℕ × ℕ, q ∈ (List.range nums.size).flatMap (fun i => (List.range i).filter (fun j => nums[i]! + nums[j]! = target) |>.map (fun j => (i, j))) → q = p := by
    have h_unique : List.length (List.flatMap (fun i => (List.range i).filter (fun j => nums[i]! + nums[j]! = target) |>.map (fun j => (i, j))) (List.range nums.size)) = 1 := by
      convert h₃ using 1;
      induction ( List.range nums.size ) <;> aesop;
    rw [ List.length_eq_one_iff ] at h_unique; obtain ⟨ p, hp ⟩ := h_unique; use p; aesop;
  obtain ⟨ ⟨ u, v ⟩, hp₁, hp₂ ⟩ := h_unique;
  simp +zetaDelta at *;
  exact ⟨ v, u, hp₁.2.1, hp₁.1, by linarith, fun a b hab hba h => by specialize hp₂ _ _ b hba a hab ( by linarith ) rfl rfl; aesop ⟩

/-
If `(u, v)` is the unique solution with `u < v`, and we start searching at row `i \le u` with sufficient fuel, `twoSum_aux` will return `#[u, v]`.
-/
theorem twoSum_aux_finds_unique (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (u v : Nat)
  (hu : u < nums.size) (hv : v < nums.size) (huv : u < v)
  (h_target : nums[u]! + nums[v]! = target)
  (h_unique : ∀ a b, a < b → b < nums.size → nums[a]! + nums[b]! = target → a = u ∧ b = v)
  (i : Nat) (hi : i ≤ u)
  (fuel : Nat) (h_fuel : fuel ≥ (u - i + 1) * nums.size) :
  twoSum_aux nums target i (i + 1) fuel = #[u, v] := by
    induction' h : u - i with d hd generalizing i fuel;
    · -- Apply the `twoSum_aux_in_row` theorem to show that the result is `#[u, v]`.
      have h_aux : twoSum_aux nums target i (i + 1) fuel = #[i, v] := by
        have h_no_sol : ∀ k, i < k → k < v → nums[i]! + nums[k]! ≠ target := by
          grind
        apply twoSum_aux_in_row;
        any_goals omega;
        · grind;
        · nlinarith [ Nat.sub_add_cancel ( by linarith : i + 1 ≤ v ) ];
      grind;
    · -- By definition of `twoSum_aux`, we know that if `i < u`, then `twoSum_aux nums target i (i + 1) fuel` will skip row `i` and proceed to row `i + 1`.
      have h_skip : twoSum_aux nums target i (i + 1) fuel = twoSum_aux nums target (i + 1) (i + 2) (fuel - (nums.size - (i + 1) + 1)) := by
        apply twoSum_aux_skip_row;
        · intro k hk₁ hk₂ hk₃; specialize h_unique i k ( by linarith ) ( by linarith ) hk₃; omega;
        · linarith [ Nat.sub_add_cancel hi ];
        · nlinarith [ Nat.sub_add_cancel ( by linarith : i + 1 ≤ nums.size ) ];
      rw [ h_skip, hd ];
      · omega;
      · rw [ show u - ( i + 1 ) = u - i - 1 by rw [ Nat.sub_sub ] ];
        exact le_tsub_of_add_le_left ( by nlinarith only [ h_fuel, Nat.sub_add_cancel ( by linarith : 1 ≤ u - i ), Nat.sub_add_cancel ( by linarith : i + 1 ≤ nums.size ) ] );
      · omega

/-
The `twoSum` function satisfies its postcondition, meaning it returns the correct unique pair of indices.
-/
theorem twoSum_spec_satisfied (nums: Array Int) (target: Int) (h_precond : twoSum_precond (nums) (target)) :
  twoSum_postcond (nums) (target) (twoSum (nums) (target) h_precond) h_precond := by
  -- By definition of `twoSum_aux`, there exist unique indices `u` and `v` such that `u < v`, `v < nums.size`, and `nums[u]! + nums[v]! = target`.
  obtain ⟨u, v, huv₁, huv₂, huv₃⟩ : ∃ u v, u < v ∧ v < nums.size ∧ nums[u]! + nums[v]! = target ∧
    (∀ a b, a < b → b < nums.size → nums[a]! + nums[b]! = target → a = u ∧ b = v)

/-
We define the `twoSum` function and its specification.
We introduce a helper function `twoSum_aux` that mirrors the internal loop of `twoSum` but is easier to reason about.
We prove that `twoSum` is equivalent to `twoSum_aux` with appropriate initial arguments.
We prove the soundness of `twoSum_aux`: if it returns a result, that result is a valid solution.
We prove the completeness of `twoSum_aux`: if a unique solution exists, `twoSum_aux` will find it given sufficient fuel.
Finally, we prove `twoSum_spec_satisfied`, showing that `twoSum` satisfies the postcondition given the precondition.

@[reducible] def twoSum_precond (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) : Prop :=
  -- The array must have at least 2 elements
  nums.size ≥ 2 ∧
  -- There exists exactly one pair of indices whose values sum to the target
  (List.range nums.size).any (fun i =>
    (List.range i).any (fun j => nums[i]! + nums[j]! = target)) ∧
  -- No other pair sums to the target (ensuring uniqueness of solution)
  ((List.range nums.size).flatMap (fun i =>
    (List.range i).filter (fun j => nums[i]! + nums[j]! = target))).length = 1

def twoSum (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (h_precond : twoSum_precond (nums) (target)) : Array Nat :=
  let rec findIndices (i : Nat) (j : Nat) (fuel : Nat) : Array Nat :=
    match fuel with
    | 0 => #[] -- Fuel exhausted, return empty array
    | fuel+1 =>
      if i >= nums.size then
        #[] -- No solution found
      else if j >= nums.size then
        findIndices (i + 1) (i + 2) fuel -- Move to next i and reset j
      else
        if nums[i]! + nums[j]! == target then
          #[i, j] -- Found solution
        else
          findIndices i (j + 1) fuel -- Try next j
   findIndices 0 1 (nums.size * nums.size)

@[reducible] def twoSum_postcond (nums : Array Int) (target : Int) (result: Array Nat) (h_precond : twoSum_precond (nums) (target)) : Prop :=
  -- Result contains exactly 2 indices
  result.size = 2 ∧
  -- The indices are valid (within bounds of the nums array)
  result[0]! < nums.size ∧ result[1]! < nums.size ∧
  -- The indices are in ascending order (sorted)
  result[0]! < result[1]! ∧
  -- The values at these indices sum to the target
  nums[result[0]!]! + nums[result[1]!]! = target

/-
This module implements a solution to the Two Sum problem in Lean 4.
It defines `twoSum` which takes a list of integers and a target integer, along with a proof that a unique solution exists.
It returns a pair of indices such that the sum of the numbers at those indices equals the target.
The correctness of the implementation is proven in `twoSum_spec_satisfied`.
-/

import Mathlib

set_option linter.mathlibStandardSet false

open scoped BigOperators
open scoped Real
open scoped Nat
open scoped Classical
open scoped Pointwise

set_option maxHeartbeats 0
set_option maxRecDepth 4000
set_option synthInstance.maxHeartbeats 20000
set_option synthInstance.maxSize 128

set_option relaxedAutoImplicit false
set_option autoImplicit false

noncomputable section

@[reducible]
def twoSum_precond (nums : List Int) (target : Int) : Prop :=
  let pairwiseSum := List.range nums.length |>.flatMap (fun i =>
    nums.drop (i + 1) |>.map (fun y => nums[i]! + y))
  nums.length > 1 ∧ pairwiseSum.count target = 1

def findComplement (nums : List Int) (target : Int) (i : Nat) (x : Int) : Option Nat :=
  let rec aux (nums : List Int) (j : Nat) : Option Nat :=
    match nums with
    | []      => none
    | y :: ys => if x + y = target then some (i + j + 1) else aux ys (j + 1)
  aux nums 0

def twoSumAux (nums : List Int) (target : Int) (i : Nat) : Prod Nat Nat :=
  match nums with
  | []      => panic! "No solution exists"
  | x :: xs =>
    match findComplement xs target i x with
    | some j => (i, j)
    | none   => twoSumAux xs target (i + 1)

def twoSum (nums : List Int) (target : Int) (h_precond : twoSum_precond (nums) (target)) : Prod Nat Nat :=
  twoSumAux nums target 0

@[reducible]
def twoSum_postcond (nums : List Int) (target : Int) (result: Prod Nat Nat) (h_precond : twoSum_precond (nums) (target)) : Prop :=
  let i := result.fst;
  let j := result.snd;
  (i < j) ∧
  (i < nums.length) ∧ (j < nums.length) ∧
  (nums[i]!) + (nums[j]!) = target

lemma findComplement_some {xs : List Int} {target : Int} {i : Nat} {x : Int} {j : Nat} :
  findComplement xs target i x = some j → i < j ∧ j <= i + xs.length ∧ ∃ k, k < xs.length ∧ j = i + 1 + k ∧ x + xs[k]! = target := by
  -- By definition of `findComplement`, if it returns `some j`, then `j` is the index of the complement of `x` in `xs`.
  intro h_findComplement
  obtain ⟨k, hk⟩ : ∃ k < xs.length, j = i + 1 + k ∧ x + xs[k]! = target := by
    -- By definition of `findComplement`, if it returns `some j`, then there exists some `k` in `xs` where `x + k = target` and `j` is the index of that `k` in `xs`. This follows directly from the definition of `findComplement`.
    have h_findComplement_spec : ∀ {xs : List ℤ} {target : ℤ} {i : ℕ} {x : ℤ} {j : ℕ}, findComplement xs target i x = some j → List.length xs ≥ 1 ∧ ∃ k < List.length xs, j = i + 1 + k ∧ x + xs[k]! = target := by
      intros xs target i x j h_findComplement; induction' xs with xs_hd xs_tl ih generalizing i x j <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement ] ;
      · cases h_findComplement;
      · by_cases h : x + xs_hd = target <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement.aux ];
        obtain ⟨ k, hk₁, hk₂, hk₃ ⟩ := ih ( show findComplement.aux target ( i + 1 ) x xs_tl 0 = Option.some j from by
                                              convert h_findComplement using 1;
                                              -- By definition of `findComplement.aux`, we can prove this by induction on the list `xs_tl`.
                                              have h_aux_eq : ∀ {xs : List ℤ} {target : ℤ} {i : ℕ} {x : ℤ} {j : ℕ}, findComplement.aux target (i + 1) x xs j = findComplement.aux target i x xs (j + 1) := by

  findComplement xs target i x = some j → i < j ∧ j <= i + xs.length ∧ ∃ k, k < xs.length ∧ j = i + 1 + k ∧ x + xs[k]! = target := by

  intro h_findComplement
  obtain ⟨k, hk⟩ : ∃ k < xs.length, j = i + 1 + k ∧ x + xs[k]! = target := by
    -- By definition of `findComplement`, if it returns `some j`, then there exists some `k` in `xs` where `x + k = target` and `j` is the index of that `k` in `xs`. This follows directly from the definition of `findComplement`.

      · cases h_findComplement;
      · by_cases h : x + xs_hd = target <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement.aux ];

                                                intros xs target i x j; induction' xs with xs_hd xs_tl ih generalizing i x j <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement.aux ] ;

                                              exact h_aux_eq );
        exact ⟨ hk₁ + 1, by linarith, by linarith, by simpa [ List.getElem?_cons ] using hk₃.2 ⟩;
    exact h_findComplement_spec h_findComplement |>.2;
  exact ⟨ by linarith, by linarith, k, hk.1, hk.2.1, hk.2.2 ⟩

  intro h_findComplement
  obtain ⟨k, hk⟩ : ∃ k < xs.length, j = i + 1 + k ∧ x + xs[k]! = target := by
    -- By definition of `findComplement`, if it returns `some j`, then there exists some `k` in `xs` where `x + k = target` and `j` is the index of that `k` in `xs`. This follows directly from the definition of `findComplement`.
    have h_findComplement_spec : ∀ {xs : List ℤ} {target : ℤ} {i : ℕ} {x : ℤ} {j : ℕ}, findComplement xs target i x = some j → List.length xs ≥ 1 ∧ ∃ k < List.length xs, j = i + 1 + k ∧ x + xs[k]! = target := by

      · cases h_findComplement;
      · by_cases h : x + xs_hd = target <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement.aux ];
        obtain ⟨ k, hk₁, hk₂, hk₃ ⟩ := ih ( show findComplement.aux target ( i + 1 ) x xs_tl 0 = Option.some j from by
                                              convert h_findComplement using 1;

                                              have h_aux_eq : ∀ {xs : List ℤ} {target : ℤ} {i : ℕ} {x : ℤ} {j : ℕ}, findComplement.aux target (i + 1) x xs j = findComplement.aux target i x xs (j + 1) := by

lemma findComplement_none {xs : List Int} {target : Int} {i : Nat} {x : Int} :
  findComplement xs target i x = none → ∀ k, k < xs.length → x + xs[k]! ≠ target := by
  intro h k hk_lt hk_eq
  have h_aux_none : ∀ (xs : List Int) (j : Nat), findComplement.aux target i x xs j = none → ∀ k, k < xs.length → x + xs[k]! ≠ target := by
    -- By induction on the list xs, we can show that if findComplement.aux returns none, then there's no index k in xs where x + xs[k]! equals the target.
    intros xs j hj_none k hk_lt hk_eq
    induction' xs with y ys ih generalizing j k;
    · contradiction;
    · rcases k with ( _ | k ) <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement.aux ];
      grind;
  -- Apply the lemma h_aux_none to the hypothesis h.
  apply h_aux_none xs 0 h k hk_lt hk_eq

lemma findComplement_eq_none {xs : List Int} {target : Int} {i : Nat} {x : Int} :
  (∀ k, k < xs.length → x + xs[k]! ≠ target) → findComplement xs target i x = none := by
  -- By definition of `findComplement`, if no element in `xs` satisfies `x + y = target`, then `findComplement xs target i x` returns `none`.
  intros h_no_complement
  induction' xs with y ys ih generalizing i x <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement ];
  · -- In the base case, when the list is empty, the function returns none.
    simp [findComplement.aux];
  · -- By definition of `findComplement.aux`, if no element in `y :: ys` satisfies `x + y = target`, then `findComplement.aux target i x (y :: ys) 0` returns `none`.
    have h_aux_none : ∀ {j : ℕ} {xs : List ℤ}, (∀ k < xs.length, x + xs[k]! ≠ target) → findComplement.aux target i x xs j = none := by
      intros j xs h_no_complement; induction' xs with y ys ih generalizing j <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement.aux ] ;
      -- Apply the induction hypothesis to the list `ys` and the parameter `j + 1`.
      have h_ind : findComplement.aux target i x ys (j + 1) = none := by

This module implements a solution to the Two Sum problem in Lean 4.

        exact ⟨ hk₁ + 1, by linarith, by linarith, by simpa [ List.getElem?_cons ] using hk₃.2 ⟩;

lemma findComplement_none {xs : List Int} {target : Int} {i : Nat} {x : Int} :
  findComplement xs target i x = none → ∀ k, k < xs.length → x + xs[k]! ≠ target := by
  intro h k hk_lt hk_eq
  have h_aux_none : ∀ (xs : List Int) (j : Nat), findComplement.aux target i x xs j = none → ∀ k, k < xs.length → x + xs[k]! ≠ target := by
    -- By induction on the list xs, we can show that if findComplement.aux returns none, then there's no index k in xs where x + xs[k]! equals the target.
    intros xs j hj_none k hk_lt hk_eq
    induction' xs with y ys ih generalizing j k;
    · contradiction;
    · rcases k with ( _ | k ) <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement.aux ];
      grind;
  -- Apply the lemma h_aux_none to the hypothesis h.
  apply h_aux_none xs 0 h k hk_lt hk_eq

lemma findComplement_eq_none {xs : List Int} {target : Int} {i : Nat} {x : Int} :
  (∀ k, k < xs.length → x + xs[k]! ≠ target) → findComplement xs target i x = none := by
  -- By definition of `findComplement`, if no element in `xs` satisfies `x + y = target`, then `findComplement xs target i x` returns `none`.
  intros h_no_complement
  induction' xs with y ys ih generalizing i x <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement ];
  · -- In the base case, when the list is empty, the function returns none.
    simp [findComplement.aux];
  · -- By definition of `findComplement.aux`, if no element in `y :: ys` satisfies `x + y = target`, then `findComplement.aux target i x (y :: ys) 0` returns `none`.
    have h_aux_none : ∀ {j : ℕ} {xs : List ℤ}, (∀ k < xs.length, x + xs[k]! ≠ target) → findComplement.aux target i x xs j = none := by
      intros j xs h_no_complement; induction' xs with y ys ih generalizing j <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement.aux ] ;
      -- Apply the induction hypothesis to the list `ys` and the parameter `j + 1`.
      have h_ind : findComplement.aux target i x ys (j + 1) = none := by
        exact ih fun k hk => h_no_complement ( k + 1 ) ( by linarith );
      exact if_neg ( h_no_complement 0 ( Nat.zero_lt_succ _ ) ) |> fun h => h.trans h_ind;
    grind

lemma twoSumAux_prop (nums : List Int) (target : Int) (i : Nat) :
  let (u, v) := twoSumAux nums target i
  u < v → i ≤ u ∧ v < i + nums.length ∧ nums[u - i]! + nums[v - i]! = target := by
  induction' nums with x xs ih generalizing i <;> simp_all +decide [ List.range_succ_eq_map ];
  · exact fun h => False.elim <| h.not_le <| by rfl;
  · by_cases h : findComplement xs target i x = none <;> simp_all +decide [ twoSumAux ];
    · grind +ring;
    · -- Since `findComplement` returns a `j`, we know that `x + xs[j - i]! = target`.
      obtain ⟨j, hj⟩ : ∃ j, findComplement xs target i x = some j := by

It returns a pair of indices such that the sum of the numbers at those indices equals the target.
The correctness of the implementation is proven in `twoSum_spec_satisfied`.
-/

import Mathlib

set_option linter.mathlibStandardSet false

open scoped BigOperators
open scoped Real
open scoped Nat
open scoped Classical
open scoped Pointwise

set_option maxHeartbeats 0
set_option maxRecDepth 4000
set_option synthInstance.maxHeartbeats 20000
set_option synthInstance.maxSize 128

set_option relaxedAutoImplicit false
set_option autoImplicit false

noncomputable section

@[reducible]
def twoSum_precond (nums : List Int) (target : Int) : Prop :=
  let pairwiseSum := List.range nums.length |>.flatMap (fun i =>
    nums.drop (i + 1) |>.map (fun y => nums[i]! + y))
  nums.length > 1 ∧ pairwiseSum.count target = 1

def findComplement (nums : List Int) (target : Int) (i : Nat) (x : Int) : Option Nat :=
  let rec aux (nums : List Int) (j : Nat) : Option Nat :=
    match nums with
    | []      => none
    | y :: ys => if x + y = target then some (i + j + 1) else aux ys (j + 1)
  aux nums 0

def twoSumAux (nums : List Int) (target : Int) (i : Nat) : Prod Nat Nat :=
  match nums with
  | []      => panic! "No solution exists"
  | x :: xs =>
    match findComplement xs target i x with
    | some j => (i, j)
    | none   => twoSumAux xs target (i + 1)

def twoSum (nums : List Int) (target : Int) (h_precond : twoSum_precond (nums) (target)) : Prod Nat Nat :=
  twoSumAux nums target 0

@[reducible]
def twoSum_postcond (nums : List Int) (target : Int) (result: Prod Nat Nat) (h_precond : twoSum_precond (nums) (target)) : Prop :=
  let i := result.fst;
  let j := result.snd;
  (i < j) ∧
  (i < nums.length) ∧ (j < nums.length) ∧
  (nums[i]!) + (nums[j]!) = target

lemma findComplement_some {xs : List Int} {target : Int} {i : Nat} {x : Int} {j : Nat} :
  findComplement xs target i x = some j → i < j ∧ j <= i + xs.length ∧ ∃ k, k < xs.length ∧ j = i + 1 + k ∧ x + xs[k]! = target := by
  -- By definition of `findComplement`, if it returns `some j`, then `j` is the index of the complement of `x` in `xs`.
  intro h_findComplement
  obtain ⟨k, hk⟩ : ∃ k < xs.length, j = i + 1 + k ∧ x + xs[k]! = target := by
    -- By definition of `findComplement`, if it returns `some j`, then there exists some `k` in `xs` where `x + k = target` and `j` is the index of that `k` in `xs`. This follows directly from the definition of `findComplement`.
    have h_findComplement_spec : ∀ {xs : List ℤ} {target : ℤ} {i : ℕ} {x : ℤ} {j : ℕ}, findComplement xs target i x = some j → List.length xs ≥ 1 ∧ ∃ k < List.length xs, j = i + 1 + k ∧ x + xs[k]! = target := by
      intros xs target i x j h_findComplement; induction' xs with xs_hd xs_tl ih generalizing i x j <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement ] ;
      · cases h_findComplement;
      · by_cases h : x + xs_hd = target <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement.aux ];
        obtain ⟨ k, hk₁, hk₂, hk₃ ⟩ := ih ( show findComplement.aux target ( i + 1 ) x xs_tl 0 = Option.some j from by
                                              convert h_findComplement using 1;
                                              -- By definition of `findComplement.aux`, we can prove this by induction on the list `xs_tl`.
                                              have h_aux_eq : ∀ {xs : List ℤ} {target : ℤ} {i : ℕ} {x : ℤ} {j : ℕ}, findComplement.aux target (i + 1) x xs j = findComplement.aux target i x xs (j + 1) := by
                                                intros xs target i x j; induction' xs with xs_hd xs_tl ih generalizing i x j <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement.aux ] ;
                                                ring;
                                              exact h_aux_eq );
        exact ⟨ hk₁ + 1, by linarith, by linarith, by simpa [ List.getElem?_cons ] using hk₃.2 ⟩;
    exact h_findComplement_spec h_findComplement |>.2;
  exact ⟨ by linarith, by linarith, k, hk.1, hk.2.1, hk.2.2 ⟩

lemma findComplement_none {xs : List Int} {target : Int} {i : Nat} {x : Int} :
  findComplement xs target i x = none → ∀ k, k < xs.length → x + xs[k]! ≠ target := by
  intro h k hk_lt hk_eq
  have h_aux_none : ∀ (xs : List Int) (j : Nat), findComplement.aux target i x xs j = none → ∀ k, k < xs.length → x + xs[k]! ≠ target := by
    -- By induction on the list xs, we can show that if findComplement.aux returns none, then there's no index k in xs where x + xs[k]! equals the target.
    intros xs j hj_none k hk_lt hk_eq
    induction' xs with y ys ih generalizing j k;
    · contradiction;
    · rcases k with ( _ | k ) <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement.aux ];
      grind;
  -- Apply the lemma h_aux_none to the hypothesis h.
  apply h_aux_none xs 0 h k hk_lt hk_eq

lemma findComplement_eq_none {xs : List Int} {target : Int} {i : Nat} {x : Int} :
  (∀ k, k < xs.length → x + xs[k]! ≠ target) → findComplement xs target i x = none := by
  -- By definition of `findComplement`, if no element in `xs` satisfies `x + y = target`, then `findComplement xs target i x` returns `none`.
  intros h_no_complement
  induction' xs with y ys ih generalizing i x <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement ];
  · -- In the base case, when the list is empty, the function returns none.
    simp [findComplement.aux];
  · -- By definition of `findComplement.aux`, if no element in `y :: ys` satisfies `x + y = target`, then `findComplement.aux target i x (y :: ys) 0` returns `none`.
    have h_aux_none : ∀ {j : ℕ} {xs : List ℤ}, (∀ k < xs.length, x + xs[k]! ≠ target) → findComplement.aux target i x xs j = none := by
      intros j xs h_no_complement; induction' xs with y ys ih generalizing j <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement.aux ] ;
      -- Apply the induction hypothesis to the list `ys` and the parameter `j + 1`.
      have h_ind : findComplement.aux target i x ys (j + 1) = none := by
        exact ih fun k hk => h_no_complement ( k + 1 ) ( by linarith );
      exact if_neg ( h_no_complement 0 ( Nat.zero_lt_succ _ ) ) |> fun h => h.trans h_ind;
    grind

lemma twoSumAux_prop (nums : List Int) (target : Int) (i : Nat) :
  let (u, v) := twoSumAux nums target i
  u < v → i ≤ u ∧ v < i + nums.length ∧ nums[u - i]! + nums[v - i]! = target := by
  induction' nums with x xs ih generalizing i <;> simp_all +decide [ List.range_succ_eq_map ];
  · exact fun h => False.elim <| h.not_le <| by rfl;
  · by_cases h : findComplement xs target i x = none <;> simp_all +decide [ twoSumAux ];
    · grind +ring;
    · -- Since `findComplement` returns a `j`, we know that `x + xs[j - i]! = target`.
      obtain ⟨j, hj⟩ : ∃ j, findComplement xs target i x = some j := by
        exact Option.ne_none_iff_exists'.mp h;
      have := findComplement_some hj; simp_all +decide [ add_comm, add_left_comm, add_assoc ] ;
      grind

lemma twoSumAux_finds_solution (nums : List Int) (target : Int) (i : Nat) :
  (∃ u v, i ≤ u ∧ u < v ∧ v < i + nums.length ∧ nums[u-i]! + nums[v-i]! = target) →
  let (u, v) := twoSumAux nums target i
  u < v ∧ v < i + nums.length ∧ nums[u-i]! + nums[v-i]! = target := by
  intro h
  obtain ⟨u, v, hu, hv, hv_lt⟩ := h;
  induction' nums with x xs ih generalizing i <;> simp_all +decide;
  · linarith;
  · by_cases h : findComplement xs target i x = none;
    · rcases u' : u - i with ( _ | u' ) <;> rcases v' : v - i with ( _ | v' ) <;> simp_all +decide [ Nat.add_comm 1 ];
      · omega;
      · contrapose! h;
        intro H; have := findComplement_none H; simp_all +decide [ Nat.sub_eq_iff_eq_add ] ;
        grind;
      · omega;
      · rw [ Nat.sub_eq_iff_eq_add ] at u' v' <;> try linarith;
        specialize ih ( i + 1 ) ; simp_all +decide [ add_assoc ];
        convert ih ( by linarith ) ( by linarith ) _ using 1;
        · rw [ show twoSumAux ( x :: xs ) target i = twoSumAux xs target ( i + 1 ) from _ ];
          exact Eq.symm ( by rw [ show twoSumAux ( x :: xs ) target i = twoSumAux xs target ( i + 1 ) from by { rw [ show twoSumAux ( x :: xs ) target i = match findComplement xs target i x with | some j => ( i, j ) | none => twoSumAux xs target ( i + 1 ) from rfl ] ; aesop } ] );
        · rw [ show twoSumAux ( x :: xs ) target i = twoSumAux xs target ( i + 1 ) from ?_ ] ; ring;
          · rw [ show ( twoSumAux xs target ( 1 + i ) ).1 - i = ( twoSumAux xs target ( 1 + i ) ).1 - ( 1 + i ) + 1 from ?_, show ( twoSumAux xs target ( 1 + i ) ).2 - i = ( twoSumAux xs target ( 1 + i ) ).2 - ( 1 + i ) + 1 from ?_ ] <;> norm_num [ Nat.sub_add_comm ];
            · rw [ tsub_add_eq_add_tsub ];
              · grind;
              · have := twoSumAux_prop xs target ( 1 + i ) ; simp_all +decide [ add_comm 1 ] ;
                linarith;
            · rw [ tsub_add_eq_add_tsub ];
              · grind;
              · have := twoSumAux_prop xs target ( 1 + i ) ; simp_all +decide [ add_comm 1 ] ;
          · exact Eq.symm ( by rw [ show twoSumAux ( x :: xs ) target i = twoSumAux xs target ( i + 1 ) from by { rw [ show twoSumAux ( x :: xs ) target i = match findComplement xs target i x with | some j => ( i, j ) | none => twoSumAux xs target ( i + 1 ) from rfl ] ; aesop } ] );
        · convert hv_lt.2 using 1 ; ring;
          simp +arith +decide [ add_assoc, add_tsub_add_eq_tsub_left ];
    · obtain ⟨ j, hj ⟩ := Option.ne_none_iff_exists'.mp h;
      -- By definition of `twoSumAux`, if `findComplement xs target i x = some j`, then `twoSumAux (x :: xs) target i = (i, j)`.
      have h_twoSumAux : twoSumAux (x :: xs) target i = (i, j) := by

    | some j => (i, j)
    | none   => twoSumAux xs target (i + 1)

def twoSum (nums : List Int) (target : Int) (h_precond : twoSum_precond (nums) (target)) : Prod Nat Nat :=
  twoSumAux nums target 0

@[reducible]
def twoSum_postcond (nums : List Int) (target : Int) (result: Prod Nat Nat) (h_precond : twoSum_precond (nums) (target)) : Prop :=
  let i := result.fst;
  let j := result.snd;
  (i < j) ∧
  (i < nums.length) ∧ (j < nums.length) ∧
  (nums[i]!) + (nums[j]!) = target

lemma findComplement_some {xs : List Int} {target : Int} {i : Nat} {x : Int} {j : Nat} :
  findComplement xs target i x = some j → i < j ∧ j <= i + xs.length ∧ ∃ k, k < xs.length ∧ j = i + 1 + k ∧ x + xs[k]! = target := by
  -- By definition of `findComplement`, if it returns `some j`, then `j` is the index of the complement of `x` in `xs`.
  intro h_findComplement
  obtain ⟨k, hk⟩ : ∃ k < xs.length, j = i + 1 + k ∧ x + xs[k]! = target := by
    -- By definition of `findComplement`, if it returns `some j`, then there exists some `k` in `xs` where `x + k = target` and `j` is the index of that `k` in `xs`. This follows directly from the definition of `findComplement`.
    have h_findComplement_spec : ∀ {xs : List ℤ} {target : ℤ} {i : ℕ} {x : ℤ} {j : ℕ}, findComplement xs target i x = some j → List.length xs ≥ 1 ∧ ∃ k < List.length xs, j = i + 1 + k ∧ x + xs[k]! = target := by
      intros xs target i x j h_findComplement; induction' xs with xs_hd xs_tl ih generalizing i x j <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement ] ;
      · cases h_findComplement;
      · by_cases h : x + xs_hd = target <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement.aux ];
        obtain ⟨ k, hk₁, hk₂, hk₃ ⟩ := ih ( show findComplement.aux target ( i + 1 ) x xs_tl 0 = Option.some j from by
                                              convert h_findComplement using 1;
                                              -- By definition of `findComplement.aux`, we can prove this by induction on the list `xs_tl`.
                                              have h_aux_eq : ∀ {xs : List ℤ} {target : ℤ} {i : ℕ} {x : ℤ} {j : ℕ}, findComplement.aux target (i + 1) x xs j = findComplement.aux target i x xs (j + 1) := by
                                                intros xs target i x j; induction' xs with xs_hd xs_tl ih generalizing i x j <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement.aux ] ;
                                                ring;
                                              exact h_aux_eq );
        exact ⟨ hk₁ + 1, by linarith, by linarith, by simpa [ List.getElem?_cons ] using hk₃.2 ⟩;
    exact h_findComplement_spec h_findComplement |>.2;
  exact ⟨ by linarith, by linarith, k, hk.1, hk.2.1, hk.2.2 ⟩

lemma findComplement_none {xs : List Int} {target : Int} {i : Nat} {x : Int} :
  findComplement xs target i x = none → ∀ k, k < xs.length → x + xs[k]! ≠ target := by
  intro h k hk_lt hk_eq
  have h_aux_none : ∀ (xs : List Int) (j : Nat), findComplement.aux target i x xs j = none → ∀ k, k < xs.length → x + xs[k]! ≠ target := by
    -- By induction on the list xs, we can show that if findComplement.aux returns none, then there's no index k in xs where x + xs[k]! equals the target.
    intros xs j hj_none k hk_lt hk_eq
    induction' xs with y ys ih generalizing j k;
    · contradiction;
    · rcases k with ( _ | k ) <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement.aux ];
      grind;
  -- Apply the lemma h_aux_none to the hypothesis h.
  apply h_aux_none xs 0 h k hk_lt hk_eq

lemma findComplement_eq_none {xs : List Int} {target : Int} {i : Nat} {x : Int} :
  (∀ k, k < xs.length → x + xs[k]! ≠ target) → findComplement xs target i x = none := by
  -- By definition of `findComplement`, if no element in `xs` satisfies `x + y = target`, then `findComplement xs target i x` returns `none`.
  intros h_no_complement
  induction' xs with y ys ih generalizing i x <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement ];
  · -- In the base case, when the list is empty, the function returns none.
    simp [findComplement.aux];
  · -- By definition of `findComplement.aux`, if no element in `y :: ys` satisfies `x + y = target`, then `findComplement.aux target i x (y :: ys) 0` returns `none`.
    have h_aux_none : ∀ {j : ℕ} {xs : List ℤ}, (∀ k < xs.length, x + xs[k]! ≠ target) → findComplement.aux target i x xs j = none := by
      intros j xs h_no_complement; induction' xs with y ys ih generalizing j <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement.aux ] ;
      -- Apply the induction hypothesis to the list `ys` and the parameter `j + 1`.
      have h_ind : findComplement.aux target i x ys (j + 1) = none := by
        exact ih fun k hk => h_no_complement ( k + 1 ) ( by linarith );
      exact if_neg ( h_no_complement 0 ( Nat.zero_lt_succ _ ) ) |> fun h => h.trans h_ind;
    grind

lemma twoSumAux_prop (nums : List Int) (target : Int) (i : Nat) :
  let (u, v) := twoSumAux nums target i
  u < v → i ≤ u ∧ v < i + nums.length ∧ nums[u - i]! + nums[v - i]! = target := by
  induction' nums with x xs ih generalizing i <;> simp_all +decide [ List.range_succ_eq_map ];
  · exact fun h => False.elim <| h.not_le <| by rfl;
  · by_cases h : findComplement xs target i x = none <;> simp_all +decide [ twoSumAux ];
    · grind +ring;
    · -- Since `findComplement` returns a `j`, we know that `x + xs[j - i]! = target`.
      obtain ⟨j, hj⟩ : ∃ j, findComplement xs target i x = some j := by
        exact Option.ne_none_iff_exists'.mp h;
      have := findComplement_some hj; simp_all +decide [ add_comm, add_left_comm, add_assoc ] ;
      grind

lemma twoSumAux_finds_solution (nums : List Int) (target : Int) (i : Nat) :
  (∃ u v, i ≤ u ∧ u < v ∧ v < i + nums.length ∧ nums[u-i]! + nums[v-i]! = target) →
  let (u, v) := twoSumAux nums target i
  u < v ∧ v < i + nums.length ∧ nums[u-i]! + nums[v-i]! = target := by
  intro h
  obtain ⟨u, v, hu, hv, hv_lt⟩ := h;
  induction' nums with x xs ih generalizing i <;> simp_all +decide;
  · linarith;
  · by_cases h : findComplement xs target i x = none;
    · rcases u' : u - i with ( _ | u' ) <;> rcases v' : v - i with ( _ | v' ) <;> simp_all +decide [ Nat.add_comm 1 ];
      · omega;
      · contrapose! h;
        intro H; have := findComplement_none H; simp_all +decide [ Nat.sub_eq_iff_eq_add ] ;
        grind;
      · omega;
      · rw [ Nat.sub_eq_iff_eq_add ] at u' v' <;> try linarith;
        specialize ih ( i + 1 ) ; simp_all +decide [ add_assoc ];
        convert ih ( by linarith ) ( by linarith ) _ using 1;
        · rw [ show twoSumAux ( x :: xs ) target i = twoSumAux xs target ( i + 1 ) from _ ];
          exact Eq.symm ( by rw [ show twoSumAux ( x :: xs ) target i = twoSumAux xs target ( i + 1 ) from by { rw [ show twoSumAux ( x :: xs ) target i = match findComplement xs target i x with | some j => ( i, j ) | none => twoSumAux xs target ( i + 1 ) from rfl ] ; aesop } ] );
        · rw [ show twoSumAux ( x :: xs ) target i = twoSumAux xs target ( i + 1 ) from ?_ ] ; ring;
          · rw [ show ( twoSumAux xs target ( 1 + i ) ).1 - i = ( twoSumAux xs target ( 1 + i ) ).1 - ( 1 + i ) + 1 from ?_, show ( twoSumAux xs target ( 1 + i ) ).2 - i = ( twoSumAux xs target ( 1 + i ) ).2 - ( 1 + i ) + 1 from ?_ ] <;> norm_num [ Nat.sub_add_comm ];
            · rw [ tsub_add_eq_add_tsub ];
              · grind;
              · have := twoSumAux_prop xs target ( 1 + i ) ; simp_all +decide [ add_comm 1 ] ;
                linarith;
            · rw [ tsub_add_eq_add_tsub ];
              · grind;
              · have := twoSumAux_prop xs target ( 1 + i ) ; simp_all +decide [ add_comm 1 ] ;
          · exact Eq.symm ( by rw [ show twoSumAux ( x :: xs ) target i = twoSumAux xs target ( i + 1 ) from by { rw [ show twoSumAux ( x :: xs ) target i = match findComplement xs target i x with | some j => ( i, j ) | none => twoSumAux xs target ( i + 1 ) from rfl ] ; aesop } ] );
        · convert hv_lt.2 using 1 ; ring;
          simp +arith +decide [ add_assoc, add_tsub_add_eq_tsub_left ];
    · obtain ⟨ j, hj ⟩ := Option.ne_none_iff_exists'.mp h;
      -- By definition of `twoSumAux`, if `findComplement xs target i x = some j`, then `twoSumAux (x :: xs) target i = (i, j)`.
      have h_twoSumAux : twoSumAux (x :: xs) target i = (i, j) := by
        exact Eq.symm ( by rw [ show twoSumAux ( x :: xs ) target i = ( i, j ) from by { rw [ twoSumAux ] ; aesop } ] );
      -- Since $findComplement xs target i x = some j$, we have $i < j$ and $j \leq i + xs.length$.
      have h_j_bounds : i < j ∧ j ≤ i + xs.length := by
        exact findComplement_some hj |> fun ⟨ hj₁, hj₂, hj₃ ⟩ => ⟨ hj₁, hj₂ ⟩;
      -- By definition of `findComplement`, if `findComplement xs target i x = some j`, then `x + (xs.get! (j - (i + 1))) = target`.
      have h_findComplement : x + (xs.get! (j - (i + 1))) = target := by

/-
This module implements a solution to the Two Sum problem in Lean 4.
It defines `twoSum` which takes a list of integers and a target integer, along with a proof that a unique solution exists.
It returns a pair of indices such that the sum of the numbers at those indices equals the target.
The correctness of the implementation is proven in `twoSum_spec_satisfied`.

import Mathlib

@[reducible]
def twoSum_precond (nums : List Int) (target : Int) : Prop :=
  let pairwiseSum := List.range nums.length |>.flatMap (fun i =>
    nums.drop (i + 1) |>.map (fun y => nums[i]! + y))
  nums.length > 1 ∧ pairwiseSum.count target = 1

def findComplement (nums : List Int) (target : Int) (i : Nat) (x : Int) : Option Nat :=
  let rec aux (nums : List Int) (j : Nat) : Option Nat :=
    match nums with
    | []      => none
    | y :: ys => if x + y = target then some (i + j + 1) else aux ys (j + 1)
  aux nums 0

def twoSumAux (nums : List Int) (target : Int) (i : Nat) : Prod Nat Nat :=
  match nums with
  | []      => panic! "No solution exists"
  | x :: xs =>
    match findComplement xs target i x with
    | some j => (i, j)
    | none   => twoSumAux xs target (i + 1)

def twoSum (nums : List Int) (target : Int) (h_precond : twoSum_precond (nums) (target)) : Prod Nat Nat :=
  twoSumAux nums target 0

@[reducible]
def twoSum_postcond (nums : List Int) (target : Int) (result: Prod Nat Nat) (h_precond : twoSum_precond (nums) (target)) : Prop :=
  let i := result.fst;
  let j := result.snd;
  (i < j) ∧
  (i < nums.length) ∧ (j < nums.length) ∧
  (nums[i]!) + (nums[j]!) = target

lemma mem_pairwiseSum_iff_exists_pair {nums : List Int} {x : Int} :
  x ∈ (List.range nums.length |>.flatMap (fun i => nums.drop (i + 1) |>.map (fun y => nums[i]! + y))) ↔
  ∃ i j, i < j ∧ j < nums.length ∧ nums[i]! + nums[j]! = x := by
  constructor;
  · simp +zetaDelta at *;
    intro i hi hx;
    rw [ List.mem_iff_get ] at hx;
    obtain ⟨ n, hn ⟩ := hx;
    use i, n + i + 1;
    grind;
  · -- If there exist indices i and j such that i < j, j < nums.length, and nums[i]! + nums[j]! = x, then x is in the flatMap of the pairwise sums.
    intro h
    obtain ⟨i, j, hij, hj, hx⟩ := h
    have h_exists : x ∈ List.map (fun y => nums[i]! + y) (List.drop (i + 1) nums) := by

@[reducible]
def twoSum_precond (nums : List Int) (target : Int) : Prop :=
  let pairwiseSum := List.range nums.length |>.flatMap (fun i =>
    nums.drop (i + 1) |>.map (fun y => nums[i]! + y))
  nums.length > 1 ∧ pairwiseSum.count target = 1

def findComplement (nums : List Int) (target : Int) (i : Nat) (x : Int) : Option Nat :=
  let rec aux (nums : List Int) (j : Nat) : Option Nat :=
    match nums with
    | []      => none
    | y :: ys => if x + y = target then some (i + j + 1) else aux ys (j + 1)
  aux nums 0

def twoSumAux (nums : List Int) (target : Int) (i : Nat) : Prod Nat Nat :=
  match nums with
  | []      => panic! "No solution exists"
  | x :: xs =>
    match findComplement xs target i x with
    | some j => (i, j)
    | none   => twoSumAux xs target (i + 1)

def twoSum (nums : List Int) (target : Int) (h_precond : twoSum_precond (nums) (target)) : Prod Nat Nat :=
  twoSumAux nums target 0

@[reducible]
def twoSum_postcond (nums : List Int) (target : Int) (result: Prod Nat Nat) (h_precond : twoSum_precond (nums) (target)) : Prop :=
  let i := result.fst;
  let j := result.snd;
  (i < j) ∧
  (i < nums.length) ∧ (j < nums.length) ∧
  (nums[i]!) + (nums[j]!) = target

lemma findComplement_some {xs : List Int} {target : Int} {i : Nat} {x : Int} {j : Nat} :
  findComplement xs target i x = some j → i < j ∧ j <= i + xs.length ∧ ∃ k, k < xs.length ∧ j = i + 1 + k ∧ x + xs[k]! = target := by
  -- By definition of `findComplement`, if it returns `some j`, then `j` is the index of the complement of `x` in `xs`.
  intro h_findComplement
  obtain ⟨k, hk⟩ : ∃ k < xs.length, j = i + 1 + k ∧ x + xs[k]! = target := by
    -- By definition of `findComplement`, if it returns `some j`, then there exists some `k` in `xs` where `x + k = target` and `j` is the index of that `k` in `xs`. This follows directly from the definition of `findComplement`.
    have h_findComplement_spec : ∀ {xs : List ℤ} {target : ℤ} {i : ℕ} {x : ℤ} {j : ℕ}, findComplement xs target i x = some j → List.length xs ≥ 1 ∧ ∃ k < List.length xs, j = i + 1 + k ∧ x + xs[k]! = target := by
      intros xs target i x j h_findComplement; induction' xs with xs_hd xs_tl ih generalizing i x j <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement ] ;
      · cases h_findComplement;
      · by_cases h : x + xs_hd = target <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement.aux ];
        obtain ⟨ k, hk₁, hk₂, hk₃ ⟩ := ih ( show findComplement.aux target ( i + 1 ) x xs_tl 0 = Option.some j from by
                                              convert h_findComplement using 1;
                                              -- By definition of `findComplement.aux`, we can prove this by induction on the list `xs_tl`.
                                              have h_aux_eq : ∀ {xs : List ℤ} {target : ℤ} {i : ℕ} {x : ℤ} {j : ℕ}, findComplement.aux target (i + 1) x xs j = findComplement.aux target i x xs (j + 1) := by
                                                intros xs target i x j; induction' xs with xs_hd xs_tl ih generalizing i x j <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement.aux ] ;
                                                ring;
                                              exact h_aux_eq );
        exact ⟨ hk₁ + 1, by linarith, by linarith, by simpa [ List.getElem?_cons ] using hk₃.2 ⟩;
    exact h_findComplement_spec h_findComplement |>.2;
  exact ⟨ by linarith, by linarith, k, hk.1, hk.2.1, hk.2.2 ⟩

lemma findComplement_none {xs : List Int} {target : Int} {i : Nat} {x : Int} :
  findComplement xs target i x = none → ∀ k, k < xs.length → x + xs[k]! ≠ target := by
  intro h k hk_lt hk_eq
  have h_aux_none : ∀ (xs : List Int) (j : Nat), findComplement.aux target i x xs j = none → ∀ k, k < xs.length → x + xs[k]! ≠ target := by
    -- By induction on the list xs, we can show that if findComplement.aux returns none, then there's no index k in xs where x + xs[k]! equals the target.
    intros xs j hj_none k hk_lt hk_eq
    induction' xs with y ys ih generalizing j k;
    · contradiction;
    · rcases k with ( _ | k ) <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement.aux ];
      grind;
  -- Apply the lemma h_aux_none to the hypothesis h.
  apply h_aux_none xs 0 h k hk_lt hk_eq

lemma findComplement_eq_none {xs : List Int} {target : Int} {i : Nat} {x : Int} :
  (∀ k, k < xs.length → x + xs[k]! ≠ target) → findComplement xs target i x = none := by
  -- By definition of `findComplement`, if no element in `xs` satisfies `x + y = target`, then `findComplement xs target i x` returns `none`.
  intros h_no_complement
  induction' xs with y ys ih generalizing i x <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement ];
  · -- In the base case, when the list is empty, the function returns none.
    simp [findComplement.aux];
  · -- By definition of `findComplement.aux`, if no element in `y :: ys` satisfies `x + y = target`, then `findComplement.aux target i x (y :: ys) 0` returns `none`.
    have h_aux_none : ∀ {j : ℕ} {xs : List ℤ}, (∀ k < xs.length, x + xs[k]! ≠ target) → findComplement.aux target i x xs j = none := by
      intros j xs h_no_complement; induction' xs with y ys ih generalizing j <;> simp_all +decide [ findComplement.aux ] ;
      -- Apply the induction hypothesis to the list `ys` and the parameter `j + 1`.
      have h_ind : findComplement.aux target i x ys (j + 1) = none := by
        exact ih fun k hk => h_no_complement ( k + 1 ) ( by linarith );
      exact if_neg ( h_no_complement 0 ( Nat.zero_lt_succ _ ) ) |> fun h => h.trans h_ind;
    grind

lemma twoSumAux_prop (nums : List Int) (target : Int) (i : Nat) :
  let (u, v) := twoSumAux nums target i
  u < v → i ≤ u ∧ v < i + nums.length ∧ nums[u - i]! + nums[v - i]! = target := by
  induction' nums with x xs ih generalizing i <;> simp_all +decide [ List.range_succ_eq_map ];
  · exact fun h => False.elim <| h.not_le <| by rfl;
  · by_cases h : findComplement xs target i x = none <;> simp_all +decide [ twoSumAux ];
    · grind +ring;
    · -- Since `findComplement` returns a `j`, we know that `x + xs[j - i]! = target`.
      obtain ⟨j, hj⟩ : ∃ j, findComplement xs target i x = some j := by
        exact Option.ne_none_iff_exists'.mp h;
      have := findComplement_some hj; simp_all +decide [ add_comm, add_left_comm, add_assoc ] ;
      grind

lemma twoSumAux_finds_solution (nums : List Int) (target : Int) (i : Nat) :
  (∃ u v, i ≤ u ∧ u < v ∧ v < i + nums.length ∧ nums[u-i]! + nums[v-i]! = target) →
  let (u, v) := twoSumAux nums target i
  u < v ∧ v < i + nums.length ∧ nums[u-i]! + nums[v-i]! = target := by
  intro h
  obtain ⟨u, v, hu, hv, hv_lt⟩ := h;
  induction' nums with x xs ih generalizing i <;> simp_all +decide;
  · linarith;
  · by_cases h : findComplement xs target i x = none;
    · rcases u' : u - i with ( _ | u' ) <;> rcases v' : v - i with ( _ | v' ) <;> simp_all +decide [ Nat.add_comm 1 ];
      · omega;
      · contrapose! h;
        intro H; have := findComplement_none H; simp_all +decide [ Nat.sub_eq_iff_eq_add ] ;
        grind;
      · omega;
      · rw [ Nat.sub_eq_iff_eq_add ] at u' v' <;> try linarith;
        specialize ih ( i + 1 ) ; simp_all +decide [ add_assoc ];
        convert ih ( by linarith ) ( by linarith ) _ using 1;
        · rw [ show twoSumAux ( x :: xs ) target i = twoSumAux xs target ( i + 1 ) from _ ];
          exact Eq.symm ( by rw [ show twoSumAux ( x :: xs ) target i = twoSumAux xs target ( i + 1 ) from by { rw [ show twoSumAux ( x :: xs ) target i = match findComplement xs target i x with | some j => ( i, j ) | none => twoSumAux xs target ( i + 1 ) from rfl ] ; aesop } ] );
        · rw [ show twoSumAux ( x :: xs ) target i = twoSumAux xs target ( i + 1 ) from ?_ ] ; ring;
          · rw [ show ( twoSumAux xs target ( 1 + i ) ).1 - i = ( twoSumAux xs target ( 1 + i ) ).1 - ( 1 + i ) + 1 from ?_, show ( twoSumAux xs target ( 1 + i ) ).2 - i = ( twoSumAux xs target ( 1 + i ) ).2 - ( 1 + i ) + 1 from ?_ ] <;> norm_num [ Nat.sub_add_comm ];
            · rw [ tsub_add_eq_add_tsub ];
              · grind;
              · have := twoSumAux_prop xs target ( 1 + i ) ; simp_all +decide [ add_comm 1 ] ;
                linarith;
            · rw [ tsub_add_eq_add_tsub ];
              · grind;
              · have := twoSumAux_prop xs target ( 1 + i ) ; simp_all +decide [ add_comm 1 ] ;
          · exact Eq.symm ( by rw [ show twoSumAux ( x :: xs ) target i = twoSumAux xs target ( i + 1 ) from by { rw [ show twoSumAux ( x :: xs ) target i = match findComplement xs target i x with | some j => ( i, j ) | none => twoSumAux xs target ( i + 1 ) from rfl ] ; aesop } ] );
        · convert hv_lt.2 using 1 ; ring;
          simp +arith +decide [ add_assoc, add_tsub_add_eq_tsub_left ];
    · obtain ⟨ j, hj ⟩ := Option.ne_none_iff_exists'.mp h;
      -- By definition of `twoSumAux`, if `findComplement xs target i x = some j`, then `twoSumAux (x :: xs) target i = (i, j)`.
      have h_twoSumAux : twoSumAux (x :: xs) target i = (i, j) := by
        exact Eq.symm ( by rw [ show twoSumAux ( x :: xs ) target i = ( i, j ) from by { rw [ twoSumAux ] ; aesop } ] );
      -- Since $findComplement xs target i x = some j$, we have $i < j$ and $j \leq i + xs.length$.
      have h_j_bounds : i < j ∧ j ≤ i + xs.length := by
        exact findComplement_some hj |> fun ⟨ hj₁, hj₂, hj₃ ⟩ => ⟨ hj₁, hj₂ ⟩;
      -- By definition of `findComplement`, if `findComplement xs target i x = some j`, then `x + (xs.get! (j - (i + 1))) = target`.
      have h_findComplement : x + (xs.get! (j - (i + 1))) = target := by
        have := findComplement_some hj; aesop;
      simp_all +decide [ Nat.sub_sub ];
      exact ⟨ by linarith, by rw [ show j - i = j - ( i + 1 ) + 1 by omega ] ; aesop ⟩

lemma mem_pairwiseSum_iff_exists_pair {nums : List Int} {x : Int} :
  x ∈ (List.range nums.length |>.flatMap (fun i => nums.drop (i + 1) |>.map (fun y => nums[i]! + y))) ↔
  ∃ i j, i < j ∧ j < nums.length ∧ nums[i]! + nums[j]! = x := by
  constructor;
  · simp +zetaDelta at *;
    intro i hi hx;
    rw [ List.mem_iff_get ] at hx;
    obtain ⟨ n, hn ⟩ := hx;
    use i, n + i + 1;
    grind;
  · -- If there exist indices i and j such that i < j, j < nums.length, and nums[i]! + nums[j]! = x, then x is in the flatMap of the pairwise sums.
    intro h
    obtain ⟨i, j, hij, hj, hx⟩ := h
    have h_exists : x ∈ List.map (fun y => nums[i]! + y) (List.drop (i + 1) nums) := by
      rw [ List.mem_iff_get ];
      use ⟨ j - ( i + 1 ), by
        simp +arith +decide [ hj.le ];
        omega ⟩
      generalize_proofs at *;
      grind;
    exact List.mem_flatMap.mpr ⟨ i, List.mem_range.mpr ( by linarith ), h_exists ⟩

lemma precond_implies_exists_pair {nums : List Int} {target : Int} :
  twoSum_precond nums target → ∃ i j, i < j ∧ j < nums.length ∧ nums[i]! + nums[j]! = target := by
  -- By definition of `twoSum_precond`, if the condition holds, then there exists a unique pair (i, j) such that i < j and nums[i]! + nums[j]! = target. This follows from the fact that the count of the target in the pairwise sums is 1.
  intros h_precond
  have h_pair : ∃ i j, i < j ∧ j < nums.length ∧ nums[i]! + nums[j]! = target := by
    have h_count : (List.range nums.length |>.flatMap (fun i => nums.drop (i + 1) |>.map (fun y => nums[i]! + y))).count target = 1 := by
      grind
    have h_pair : target ∈ (List.range nums.length |>.flatMap (fun i => nums.drop (i + 1) |>.map (fun y => nums[i]! + y))) := by
      -- Since the count of the target in the list is 1, the target must be present in the list.
      have h_target_in_list : target ∈ List.flatMap (fun i => List.map (fun y => nums[i]! + y) (List.drop (i + 1) nums)) (List.range nums.length) := by
        have h_count_pos : 0 < List.count target (List.flatMap (fun i => List.map (fun y => nums[i]! + y) (List.drop (i + 1) nums)) (List.range nums.length)) := by

/-
This module implements a solution to the Two Sum problem in Lean 4.
It defines `twoSum` which takes a list of integers and a target integer, along with a proof that a unique solution exists.
It returns a pair of indices such that the sum of the numbers at those indices equals the target.
The correctness of the implementation is proven in `twoSum_spec_satisfied`.
-/
