    exact h_foldl l best h x hx

theorem foldl_updateCandidate_none (l : List (List Char)) :
  l.foldl updateCandidate none = none ↔ l = [] := by
    -- We'll use induction on the list `l`.
    induction' l with x l ih ih;
    · exact?;
    · -- By definition of `updateCandidate`, if the list is non-empty, the foldl cannot be none.
      have h_nonempty : ∀ (l : List (List Char)), l ≠ [] → List.foldl updateCandidate none l ≠ none := by
        -- By induction on the list, we can show that if the list is non-empty, the foldl will process the first element and set the accumulator to that element, then process the rest of the list.
        intro l hl_nonempty
        induction' l with x l ih;
        · contradiction;
        · induction' l using List.reverseRecOn with x l ih <;> simp_all +decide [ List.foldl ];
          · exact?;
          · unfold updateCandidate; aesop;

def allSubstrings_spec (s : List Char) : List (List Char) :=
  (List.range s.length).flatMap (fun i =>
    (List.range (s.length - i + 1)).map (fun len =>
      s.drop i |>.take len))

theorem allSubstrings_eq_filter_spec (s : List Char) :
  allSubstrings s = (allSubstrings_spec s).filter (fun x => x ≠ []) := by
    -- By definition of `allSubstrings`, we know that it is equal to `allSubstrings_spec` filtered by non-empty sublists because `allSubstrings` includes only non-empty sublists.
    simp [allSubstrings, allSubstrings_spec];
    -- The two lists are equal because the flatMap of the first list already excludes the empty lists that are present in the second list.
    have h_eq : ∀ i ∈ List.range s.length, List.map (fun j => List.take (j + 1) (List.drop i s)) (List.range (s.length - i)) = List.filter (fun x => !Decidable.decide (x = [])) (List.map (fun len => List.take len (List.drop i s)) (List.range (s.length - i + 1))) := by
      intro i hi; induction s.length - i <;> simp_all +decide [ List.range_succ_eq_map ] ;
      rw [ List.filter_eq_self.mpr ] ; aesop;
      simp_all +decide [ Function.comp ];
    -- By applying h_eq to each element in the flatMap, we can replace each map with the filtered map.
    have h_flatMap_eq : List.flatMap (fun i => List.map (fun j => List.take (j + 1) (List.drop i s)) (List.range (s.length - i))) (List.range s.length) = List.flatMap (fun i => List.filter (fun x => !Decidable.decide (x = [])) (List.map (fun len => List.take len (List.drop i s)) (List.range (s.length - i + 1)))) (List.range s.length) := by
      exact?;
    exact h_flatMap_eq.trans ( by induction ( List.range s.length ) <;> aesop )

theorem compareCandidates_false_implies_le (a b : List Char) :
  compareCandidates a b = false → b.length < a.length ∨ (b.length = a.length ∧ listToString b ≤ listToString a) := by
    -- By definition of compareCandidates, if compareCandidates a b is false, then either a.length > b.length or a.length = b.length and a is not less than b lexicographically. This follows directly from the definition of compareCandidates.
    simp [compareCandidates];
    -- We can split into cases based on whether `b.length` is less than or equal to `a.length`.
    by_cases h : b.length < a.length;
    · grind;
    · -- Since $b.length$ is not less than $a.length$, we have $b.length \geq a.length$. If $b.length = a.length$, then by the definition of compareCandidates, $a$ is not less than $b$ lexicographically.
      by_cases h_eq : b.length = a.length;
      · -- Since $a$ is not less than $b$ lexicographically, we have $b \leq a$.
        have h_lex : isLexSmaller a b = Bool.false → listToString b ≤ listToString a := by

          simp [isLexSmaller];
        aesop;
      · exact fun h' => False.elim <| h_eq <| le_antisymm h' <| le_of_not_gt h

@[reducible]
def searchInsert_precond (xs : List Int) (target : Int) : Prop :=
  List.Pairwise (· < ·) xs

def searchInsert (xs : List Int) (target : Int) (h_precond : searchInsert_precond (xs) (target)) : Nat :=
  match xs with
  | [] =>
      0
  | _ :: _ =>
      let rec helper : List Int → Nat → Nat :=
        fun ys idx =>
          match ys with
          | [] =>
              idx
          | y :: ys' =>
              let isCurrent := y
              let currentIndex := idx
              let targetValue := target
              let condition := targetValue ≤ isCurrent
              if condition then
                currentIndex
              else
                let incrementedIndex := currentIndex + 1
                let rest := ys'
                helper rest incrementedIndex
      let startingIndex := 0
      let result := helper xs startingIndex
      result

@[reducible]
def searchInsert_postcond (xs : List Int) (target : Int) (result: Nat) (h_precond : searchInsert_precond (xs) (target)) : Prop :=
  let allBeforeLess := (List.range result).all (fun i => xs[i]! < target)
  let inBounds := result ≤ xs.length
  let insertedCorrectly :=
    result < xs.length → target ≤ xs[result]!
  inBounds ∧ allBeforeLess ∧ insertedCorrectly

theorem searchInsert_spec_satisfied (xs: List Int) (target: Int) (h_precond : searchInsert_precond (xs) (target)) :
    searchInsert_postcond (xs) (target) (searchInsert (xs) (target) h_precond) h_precond := by
  unfold searchInsert_postcond searchInsert;
  -- We'll use induction on the length of the tail to prove the properties of the helper function.
  have h_ind : ∀ (xs : List ℤ) (target : ℤ) (h_precond : searchInsert_precond xs target), (∀ (i : ℕ), i < searchInsert.helper target xs 0 → (xs[i]!) < target) ∧ (searchInsert.helper target xs 0 ≤ xs.length) ∧ (searchInsert.helper target xs 0 < xs.length → target ≤ xs[(searchInsert.helper target xs 0)]!) := by
    -- We'll use induction on the length of the list to prove the properties of the helper function.
    intro xs target h_precond
    induction' xs with x xs ih generalizing target;
    · simp [searchInsert.helper];
    · -- By definition of `searchInsert.helper`, we know that it returns the correct index where the target should be inserted in the list `x :: xs`.
      simp [searchInsert.helper] at *;
      -- By definition of `searchInsert.helper`, we know that it returns the correct index where the target should be inserted in the list `x :: xs`. We can use the induction hypothesis on `xs` with the starting index 1.
      have h_ind : searchInsert.helper target xs 1 = searchInsert.helper target xs 0 + 1 := by
        have h_ind : ∀ (xs : List ℤ) (target : ℤ) (start : ℕ), searchInsert.helper target xs (start + 1) = searchInsert.helper target xs start + 1 := by

/-
This module implements a run-length encoding algorithm `runLengthEncode` and proves that it satisfies the specification `runLengthEncode_postcond`, which requires that the encoded list has no zero-length runs, no consecutive identical characters, and decodes back to the original string.

import Mathlib

set_option linter.mathlibStandardSet false

open scoped BigOperators
open scoped Real
open scoped Nat
open scoped Classical
open scoped Pointwise

set_option maxHeartbeats 0
set_option maxRecDepth 4000
set_option synthInstance.maxHeartbeats 20000
set_option synthInstance.maxSize 128

set_option relaxedAutoImplicit false
set_option autoImplicit false

@[reducible, simp]
def runLengthEncode_precond (s : String) : Prop :=
  True

def runLengthEncode (s : String) (h_precond : runLengthEncode_precond (s)) : List (Char × Nat) :=
  let chars := s.data

  let rec encodeAux (acc : List (Char × Nat)) (rest : List Char) : List (Char × Nat) :=
    match rest with
    | [] => acc.reverse
    | h :: t =>
      match acc with
      | (ch, count) :: accTail =>
        if ch = h then
          encodeAux ((ch, count + 1) :: accTail) t
        else
          encodeAux ((h, 1) :: (ch, count) :: accTail) t
      | [] =>
        encodeAux ([(h, 1)]) t

  let encoded := encodeAux [] chars
  encoded

def decodeRLE (lst : List (Char × Nat)) : String :=
  match lst with
  | [] => ""
  | (ch, cnt) :: tail =>
    let repeated := String.mk (List.replicate cnt ch)
    repeated ++ decodeRLE tail

@[reducible, simp]
def runLengthEncode_postcond (s : String) (result: List (Char × Nat)) (h_precond : runLengthEncode_precond (s)) : Prop :=
  (∀ pair ∈ result, pair.snd > 0) ∧
  (∀ i : Nat, i < result.length - 1 → (result[i]!).fst ≠ (result[i+1]!).fst) ∧
  decodeRLE result = s

theorem runLengthEncode_spec_satisfied (s: String) (h_precond : runLengthEncode_precond (s)) :
    runLengthEncode_postcond (s) (runLengthEncode (s) h_precond) h_precond := by
  refine' ⟨ _, _, _ ⟩;
  · -- By definition of `runLengthEncode`, each pair in the result has a length greater than 0.
    intros pair hpair
    apply List.mem_iff_get.mp hpair |> fun ⟨i, hi⟩ => by
      -- By definition of `runLengthEncode`, each pair in the result has a length greater than 0. We can prove this by induction on the list.
      have h_pos : ∀ (s : String), ∀ (acc : List (Char × Nat)), (∀ pair ∈ acc, pair.snd > 0) → ∀ (rest : List Char), (∀ pair ∈ runLengthEncode.encodeAux acc rest, pair.snd > 0) := by

  True

def runLengthEncode (s : String) (h_precond : runLengthEncode_precond (s)) : List (Char × Nat) :=
  let chars := s.data

  let rec encodeAux (acc : List (Char × Nat)) (rest : List Char) : List (Char × Nat) :=
    match rest with
    | [] => acc.reverse
    | h :: t =>
      match acc with
      | (ch, count) :: accTail =>
        if ch = h then
          encodeAux ((ch, count + 1) :: accTail) t
        else
          encodeAux ((h, 1) :: (ch, count) :: accTail) t
      | [] =>
        encodeAux ([(h, 1)]) t

  let encoded := encodeAux [] chars
  encoded

def decodeRLE (lst : List (Char × Nat)) : String :=
  match lst with
  | [] => ""
  | (ch, cnt) :: tail =>
    let repeated := String.mk (List.replicate cnt ch)
    repeated ++ decodeRLE tail

@[reducible, simp]
def runLengthEncode_postcond (s : String) (result: List (Char × Nat)) (h_precond : runLengthEncode_precond (s)) : Prop :=
  (∀ pair ∈ result, pair.snd > 0) ∧
  (∀ i : Nat, i < result.length - 1 → (result[i]!).fst ≠ (result[i+1]!).fst) ∧
  decodeRLE result = s

theorem runLengthEncode_spec_satisfied (s: String) (h_precond : runLengthEncode_precond (s)) :
    runLengthEncode_postcond (s) (runLengthEncode (s) h_precond) h_precond := by
  refine' ⟨ _, _, _ ⟩;
  · -- By definition of `runLengthEncode`, each pair in the result has a length greater than 0.
    intros pair hpair
    apply List.mem_iff_get.mp hpair |> fun ⟨i, hi⟩ => by
      -- By definition of `runLengthEncode`, each pair in the result has a length greater than 0. We can prove this by induction on the list.
      have h_pos : ∀ (s : String), ∀ (acc : List (Char × Nat)), (∀ pair ∈ acc, pair.snd > 0) → ∀ (rest : List Char), (∀ pair ∈ runLengthEncode.encodeAux acc rest, pair.snd > 0) := by
        intros s acc hacc rest pair hpair
        induction' rest with rest_head rest_tail ih generalizing acc;
        · -- In the base case, when the list is empty, the result is the accumulator. Since the accumulator is constructed by adding pairs with positive counts, any pair in the accumulator must have a positive count. Therefore, the base case holds.
          simp [runLengthEncode.encodeAux] at hpair;
          exact hacc _ hpair;
        · rcases acc with ( _ | ⟨ ⟨ ch, cnt ⟩, acc ⟩ ) <;> simp_all +decide [ runLengthEncode.encodeAux ];
          · grind;
          · split_ifs at hpair <;> [ exact ih _ ( by aesop ) hpair; exact ih _ ( by aesop ) hpair ];
      exact h_pos s [] ( by norm_num ) s.data _ ( hi ▸ List.getElem_mem _ );
  · -- By definition of `runLengthEncode`, the encoded list has no consecutive pairs with the same first element.
    have h_no_consecutive_pairs : ∀ (acc : List (Char × Nat)) (rest : List Char), (∀ i : ℕ, i < acc.length - 1 → acc[i]!.fst ≠ acc[i + 1]!.fst) → runLengthEncode.encodeAux acc rest = (runLengthEncode.encodeAux acc rest) → (∀ i : ℕ, i < (runLengthEncode.encodeAux acc rest).length - 1 → (runLengthEncode.encodeAux acc rest)[i]!.fst ≠ (runLengthEncode.encodeAux acc rest)[i + 1]!.fst) := by

/-
This module implements a run-length encoding algorithm `runLengthEncode` and proves that it satisfies the specification `runLengthEncode_postcond`, which requires that the encoded list has no zero-length runs, no consecutive identical characters, and decodes back to the original string.
-/

import Mathlib

set_option linter.mathlibStandardSet false

open scoped BigOperators
open scoped Real
open scoped Nat
open scoped Classical
open scoped Pointwise

set_option maxRecDepth 4000
set_option synthInstance.maxHeartbeats 20000
set_option synthInstance.maxSize 128

set_option relaxedAutoImplicit false
set_option autoImplicit false

@[reducible, simp]
def runLengthEncode_precond (s : String) : Prop :=
  True

def runLengthEncode (s : String) (h_precond : runLengthEncode_precond (s)) : List (Char × Nat) :=
  let chars := s.data

  let rec encodeAux (acc : List (Char × Nat)) (rest : List Char) : List (Char × Nat) :=
    match rest with
    | [] => acc.reverse
    | h :: t =>
      match acc with
      | (ch, count) :: accTail =>
        if ch = h then
          encodeAux ((ch, count + 1) :: accTail) t
        else
          encodeAux ((h, 1) :: (ch, count) :: accTail) t
      | [] =>
        encodeAux ([(h, 1)]) t

  let encoded := encodeAux [] chars
  encoded

def decodeRLE (lst : List (Char × Nat)) : String :=
  match lst with
  | [] => ""
  | (ch, cnt) :: tail =>
    let repeated := String.mk (List.replicate cnt ch)
    repeated ++ decodeRLE tail

@[reducible, simp]
def runLengthEncode_postcond (s : String) (result: List (Char × Nat)) (h_precond : runLengthEncode_precond (s)) : Prop :=
  (∀ pair ∈ result, pair.snd > 0) ∧
  (∀ i : Nat, i < result.length - 1 → (result[i]!).fst ≠ (result[i+1]!).fst) ∧
  decodeRLE result = s

theorem runLengthEncode_spec_satisfied (s: String) (h_precond : runLengthEncode_precond (s)) :
    runLengthEncode_postcond (s) (runLengthEncode (s) h_precond) h_precond := by
  refine' ⟨ _, _, _ ⟩;
  · -- By definition of `runLengthEncode`, each pair in the result has a length greater than 0.
    intros pair hpair
    apply List.mem_iff_get.mp hpair |> fun ⟨i, hi⟩ => by
      -- By definition of `runLengthEncode`, each pair in the result has a length greater than 0. We can prove this by induction on the list.
      have h_pos : ∀ (s : String), ∀ (acc : List (Char × Nat)), (∀ pair ∈ acc, pair.snd > 0) → ∀ (rest : List Char), (∀ pair ∈ runLengthEncode.encodeAux acc rest, pair.snd > 0) := by
        intros s acc hacc rest pair hpair
        induction' rest with rest_head rest_tail ih generalizing acc;
        · -- In the base case, when the list is empty, the result is the accumulator. Since the accumulator is constructed by adding pairs with positive counts, any pair in the accumulator must have a positive count. Therefore, the base case holds.
          simp [runLengthEncode.encodeAux] at hpair;
          exact hacc _ hpair;
        · rcases acc with ( _ | ⟨ ⟨ ch, cnt ⟩, acc ⟩ ) <;> simp_all +decide [ runLengthEncode.encodeAux ];
          · grind;
          · split_ifs at hpair <;> [ exact ih _ ( by aesop ) hpair; exact ih _ ( by aesop ) hpair ];
      exact h_pos s [] ( by norm_num ) s.data _ ( hi ▸ List.getElem_mem _ );
  · -- By definition of `runLengthEncode`, the encoded list has no consecutive pairs with the same first element.
    have h_no_consecutive_pairs : ∀ (acc : List (Char × Nat)) (rest : List Char), (∀ i : ℕ, i < acc.length - 1 → acc[i]!.fst ≠ acc[i + 1]!.fst) → runLengthEncode.encodeAux acc rest = (runLengthEncode.encodeAux acc rest) → (∀ i : ℕ, i < (runLengthEncode.encodeAux acc rest).length - 1 → (runLengthEncode.encodeAux acc rest)[i]!.fst ≠ (runLengthEncode.encodeAux acc rest)[i + 1]!.fst) := by
      intro acc rest h_no_consecutive_pairs h_encodeAux
      induction' rest with ch rest ih generalizing acc;
      · simp_all +decide [ runLengthEncode.encodeAux ];
        intro i hi;
        convert h_no_consecutive_pairs ( acc.length - 2 - i ) _ using 1;
        · rw [ List.getElem?_reverse, List.getElem?_reverse ];
          · rw [ show acc.length - 1 - i = acc.length - 2 - i + 1 from by omega, show acc.length - 1 - ( i + 1 ) = acc.length - 2 - i from by omega ];
            rw [ eq_comm ];
          · omega;
          · exact lt_of_lt_of_le hi ( Nat.pred_le _ );
        · omega;
      · rcases acc with ( _ | ⟨ ⟨ ch', n ⟩, acc ⟩ ) <;> simp_all +decide [ runLengthEncode.encodeAux ];
        split_ifs <;> [ exact ih _ ( by
          grind ) ; exact ih _ ( by
          grind ) ];
    exact h_no_consecutive_pairs _ _ ( by norm_num ) rfl;
  · -- By definition of `runLengthEncode.encodeAux`, we can prove this by induction on the list of characters.
    have h_encode : ∀ (acc : List (Char × Nat)) (chars : List Char), decodeRLE (runLengthEncode.encodeAux acc chars) = String.mk (List.flatMap (fun (p : Char × Nat) => List.replicate p.snd p.fst) acc).reverse ++ String.mk chars := by
      -- We'll use induction on the list of characters to prove the equality.
      intro acc chars
      induction' chars with h t ih generalizing acc;
      · -- By definition of `decodeRLE`, we can rewrite the goal using the definition of `decodeRLE`.
        have h_decode_def : ∀ (acc : List (Char × ℕ)), decodeRLE (acc.reverse) = String.mk (List.flatMap (fun (p : Char × ℕ) => List.replicate p.snd p.fst) acc.reverse) := by
          intro acc
          induction' acc.reverse with p acc ih;
          · rfl;
          · -- By definition of `decodeRLE`, we can rewrite the goal using the definition of `decodeRLE` for the cons case.
            have h_decode_def : ∀ (p : Char × ℕ) (acc : List (Char × ℕ)), decodeRLE (p :: acc) = String.mk (List.replicate p.snd p.fst) ++ decodeRLE acc := by

/-
This module defines a function `reverseWords` that reverses the order of words in a given string, removing extra spaces. It also includes a formal specification `reverseWords_postcond` and a proof `reverseWords_spec_satisfied` that the function satisfies this specification.
-/

import Mathlib

set_option linter.mathlibStandardSet false

open scoped BigOperators
open scoped Real
open scoped Nat
open scoped Classical
open scoped Pointwise

set_option maxHeartbeats 0
set_option maxRecDepth 4000
set_option synthInstance.maxHeartbeats 20000
set_option synthInstance.maxSize 128

set_option relaxedAutoImplicit false
set_option autoImplicit false

noncomputable section

@[reducible]
def reverseWords_precond (words_str : String) : Prop :=
  True

def reverseWords (words_str : String) (h_precond : reverseWords_precond (words_str)) : String :=
  let rawWords : List String := words_str.splitOn " "
  let rec filterNonEmpty (words : List String) : List String :=
    match words with
    | [] => []
    | h :: t =>
      if h = "" then
        filterNonEmpty t
      else
        h :: filterNonEmpty t
  let filteredWords : List String := filterNonEmpty rawWords
  let revWords : List String := filteredWords.reverse
  let rec joinWithSpace (words : List String) : String :=
    match words with
    | [] => ""
    | [w] => w
    | h :: t =>
      h ++ " " ++ joinWithSpace t
  let result : String := joinWithSpace revWords
  result

@[reducible]
def reverseWords_postcond (words_str : String) (result: String) (h_precond : reverseWords_precond (words_str)) : Prop :=
  ∃ words : List String,
    (words = (words_str.splitOn " ").filter (fun w => w ≠ "")) ∧
    result = String.intercalate " " (words.reverse)

theorem reverseWords_spec_satisfied (words_str: String) (h_precond : reverseWords_precond (words_str)) :
    reverseWords_postcond (words_str) (reverseWords (words_str) h_precond) h_precond := by
  -- By definition of `joinWithSpace`, we know that `joinWithSpace (filterNonEmpty (splitOn words_str))` is equal to `intercalate " " (filterNonEmpty (splitOn words_str))`.
  have h_join : ∀ (words : List String), reverseWords.joinWithSpace words = String.intercalate " " words := by
    -- By definition of `joinWithSpace`, we can show that it is equivalent to `String.intercalate` by induction on the list of words.
    intro words
    induction' words with w words ih;
    · rfl;
    · induction' words with w' words' ih' <;> simp_all +decide [ reverseWords.joinWithSpace ];
      · exact?;
      · -- By definition of `intercalate`, we can split the list into the first element and the rest.
        have h_intercalate_split : ∀ (w : String) (ws : List String), String.intercalate " " (w :: ws) = w ++ List.foldr (fun s acc => " " ++ s ++ acc) "" ws := by

import Mathlib

set_option linter.mathlibStandardSet false

@[reducible]
def reverseWords_precond (words_str : String) : Prop :=
  True

def reverseWords (words_str : String) (h_precond : reverseWords_precond (words_str)) : String :=
  let rawWords : List String := words_str.splitOn " "
  let rec filterNonEmpty (words : List String) : List String :=
    match words with
    | [] => []
    | h :: t =>
      if h = "" then
        filterNonEmpty t
      else
        h :: filterNonEmpty t
  let filteredWords : List String := filterNonEmpty rawWords
  let revWords : List String := filteredWords.reverse
  let rec joinWithSpace (words : List String) : String :=
    match words with
    | [] => ""
    | [w] => w
    | h :: t =>
      h ++ " " ++ joinWithSpace t
  let result : String := joinWithSpace revWords
  result

@[reducible]
def reverseWords_postcond (words_str : String) (result: String) (h_precond : reverseWords_precond (words_str)) : Prop :=
  ∃ words : List String,
    (words = (words_str.splitOn " ").filter (fun w => w ≠ "")) ∧
    result = String.intercalate " " (words.reverse)

theorem reverseWords_spec_satisfied (words_str: String) (h_precond : reverseWords_precond (words_str)) :
    reverseWords_postcond (words_str) (reverseWords (words_str) h_precond) h_precond := by
  -- By definition of `joinWithSpace`, we know that `joinWithSpace (filterNonEmpty (splitOn words_str))` is equal to `intercalate " " (filterNonEmpty (splitOn words_str))`.
  have h_join : ∀ (words : List String), reverseWords.joinWithSpace words = String.intercalate " " words := by
    -- By definition of `joinWithSpace`, we can show that it is equivalent to `String.intercalate` by induction on the list of words.
    intro words
    induction' words with w words ih;
    · rfl;
    · induction' words with w' words' ih' <;> simp_all +decide [ reverseWords.joinWithSpace ];
      · exact?;
      · -- By definition of `intercalate`, we can split the list into the first element and the rest.
        have h_intercalate_split : ∀ (w : String) (ws : List String), String.intercalate " " (w :: ws) = w ++ List.foldr (fun s acc => " " ++ s ++ acc) "" ws := by
          intros w ws; induction ws generalizing w <;> simp +decide [ *, String.intercalate ] ;
          · rfl;
          · rename_i k hk ih; specialize ih ( w ++ " " ++ k ) ; simp_all +decide [ String.intercalate ] ;
            convert ih using 1;
            simp +decide [ String.ext_iff, List.foldr ];
        simp +decide [ h_intercalate_split, String.ext_iff ];
  -- By definition of `filterNonEmpty`, we know that `filterNonEmpty (splitOn words_str)` is equal to `filter (fun w => w ≠ "") (splitOn words_str)`.
  have h_filter : ∀ (words : List String), reverseWords.filterNonEmpty words = List.filter (fun w => w ≠ "") words := by

/-
We defined a function `reverseString` that reverses a string using a tail-recursive helper function. We also defined a precondition `reverseString_precond` (which is trivial) and a postcondition `reverseString_postcond` asserting that the result has the same length as the input and its characters are in reverse order. Finally, we proved `reverseString_spec_satisfied`, showing that `reverseString` satisfies the postcondition.
-/

import Mathlib

set_option linter.mathlibStandardSet false

open scoped BigOperators
open scoped Real
open scoped Nat
open scoped Classical
open scoped Pointwise

set_option maxHeartbeats 0
set_option maxRecDepth 4000
set_option synthInstance.maxHeartbeats 20000
set_option synthInstance.maxSize 128

set_option relaxedAutoImplicit false
set_option autoImplicit false

noncomputable section

@[reducible]
def reverseString_precond (s : String) : Prop :=
  True

def reverseString (s : String) (h_precond : reverseString_precond (s)) : String :=
  let rec reverseAux (chars : List Char) (acc : List Char) : List Char :=
    match chars with
    | [] => acc
    | h::t => reverseAux t (h::acc)
  String.mk (reverseAux (s.toList) [])

@[reducible]
def reverseString_postcond (s : String) (result: String) (h_precond : reverseString_precond (s)) : Prop :=
  result.length = s.length ∧ result.toList = s.toList.reverse

theorem reverseString_spec_satisfied (s: String) (h_precond : reverseString_precond (s)) :
    reverseString_postcond (s) (reverseString (s) h_precond) h_precond := by
  -- By definition of `reverseString`, we know that `reverseString s h_precond` is the reversed version of `s`.
  have h_rev : reverseString s h_precond = String.mk (s.toList.reverse) := by
    -- By definition of `reverseString`, we know that `reverseString s h_precond` is the reversed version of `s` because it applies `List.reverse` to the list of characters.
    simp [reverseString];
    -- By definition of `reverseAux`, we have `reverseAux s.data [] = s.data.reverse` because `reverseAux` is equivalent to `List.reverse`.
    have h_reverse_aux : ∀ (l : List Char) (acc : List Char), reverseString.reverseAux l acc = l.reverse ++ acc := by
      -- We can prove this by induction on the list `l`.
      intro l acc
      induction' l with h t ih generalizing acc;
      · rfl;
      · -- By definition of `reverseAux`, we have `reverseString.reverseAux (h :: t) acc = reverseString.reverseAux t (h :: acc)`.
        have h_def : reverseString.reverseAux (h :: t) acc = reverseString.reverseAux t (h :: acc) := by

/-
We define `removeElement` which removes all occurrences of a target number from a list of natural numbers. We prove `removeElement_spec_satisfied`, showing that the function correctly filters out the target element while preserving the order of other elements.
-/

import Mathlib

set_option linter.mathlibStandardSet false

open scoped BigOperators
open scoped Real
open scoped Nat
open scoped Classical
open scoped Pointwise

set_option maxHeartbeats 0
set_option maxRecDepth 4000
set_option synthInstance.maxHeartbeats 20000
set_option synthInstance.maxSize 128

set_option relaxedAutoImplicit false
set_option autoImplicit false

noncomputable section

@[reducible]
def removeElement_precond (lst : List Nat) (target : Nat) : Prop :=
  True

def removeElement (lst : List Nat) (target : Nat) (h_precond : removeElement_precond (lst) (target)) : List Nat :=
  let rec helper (lst : List Nat) (target : Nat) : List Nat :=
    match lst with
    | [] => []
    | x :: xs =>
      let rest := helper xs target
      if x = target then rest else x :: rest
  helper lst target

@[reducible]
def removeElement_postcond (lst : List Nat) (target : Nat) (result: List Nat) (h_precond : removeElement_precond (lst) (target)): Prop :=
  let lst' := lst.filter (fun x => x ≠ target)
  result.zipIdx.all (fun (x, i) =>
    match lst'[i]? with
    | some y => x = y
    | none => false) ∧ result.length = lst'.length

theorem removeElement_spec_satisfied (lst: List Nat) (target: Nat) (h_precond : removeElement_precond (lst) (target)):
    removeElement_postcond (lst) (target) (removeElement (lst) (target) h_precond) h_precond := by
  -- By definition of `removeElement`, we know that `removeElement lst target h_precond` is equivalent to `lst.filter (fun x => x ≠ target)`.
  have h_eq : removeElement lst target h_precond = lst.filter (fun x => x ≠ target) := by
    -- By definition of `removeElement.helper`, we can prove this by induction on `lst`.
    have h_helper : ∀ (lst : List ℕ) (target : ℕ), removeElement.helper lst target = lst.filter (fun x => x ≠ target) := by

/-
This module implements a function `removeDuplicates` that counts the number of unique elements in a sorted list of integers. It defines a precondition `removeDuplicates_precond` ensuring the input list is sorted, and a postcondition `removeDuplicates_postcond` asserting that the result matches the length of the list with duplicates removed. Finally, it proves `removeDuplicates_spec_satisfied`, confirming that the implementation satisfies the postcondition given the precondition.
-/

import Mathlib

set_option linter.mathlibStandardSet false

open scoped BigOperators
open scoped Real
open scoped Nat
open scoped Classical
open scoped Pointwise

set_option maxHeartbeats 0
set_option maxRecDepth 4000
set_option synthInstance.maxHeartbeats 20000
set_option synthInstance.maxSize 128

set_option relaxedAutoImplicit false
set_option autoImplicit false

noncomputable section

@[reducible]
def removeDuplicates_precond (nums : List Int) : Prop :=
  -- !benchmark @start precond
  -- nums are sorted in non-decreasing order
  List.Pairwise (· ≤ ·) nums
  -- !benchmark @end precond

def removeDuplicates (nums : List Int) (h_precond : removeDuplicates_precond (nums)) : Nat :=
  -- !benchmark @start code
  match nums with
  | [] =>
    0
  | h :: t =>
    let init := h
    let initCount := 1
    let rec countUniques (prev : Int) (xs : List Int) (k : Nat) : Nat :=
      match xs with
      | [] =>
        k
      | head :: tail =>
        let isDuplicate := head = prev
        if isDuplicate then
          countUniques prev tail k
        else
          let newK := k + 1
          countUniques head tail newK
    countUniques init t initCount
  -- !benchmark @end code

@[reducible]
def removeDuplicates_postcond (nums : List Int) (result: Nat) (h_precond : removeDuplicates_precond (nums)) : Prop :=
  -- !benchmark @start postcond
  result - nums.eraseDups.length = 0 ∧
  nums.eraseDups.length ≤ result
  -- !benchmark @end postcond

theorem removeDuplicates_spec_satisfied (nums: List Int) (h_precond : removeDuplicates_precond (nums)) :
    removeDuplicates_postcond (nums) (removeDuplicates (nums) h_precond) h_precond := by
  -- !benchmark @start proof
  -- By definition of `removeDuplicates`, we know that `removeDuplicates nums h_precond` is equal to `nums.eraseDups.length`.
  have h_eq : removeDuplicates nums h_precond = nums.eraseDups.length := by
    unfold removeDuplicates;
    induction' nums with h t ih ; aesop;
    rcases t with ( _ | ⟨ h', t' ⟩ ) <;> simp_all +decide;
    · rfl;
    · -- By definition of `countUniques`, we can split into cases based on whether `h` equals `h'`.
      by_cases h_eq : h = h';
      · simp_all +decide [ removeDuplicates.countUniques, List.eraseDups_cons ];
      · -- Since h is not equal to h', the countUniques function will add 1 to the count and then proceed with the rest of the list.
        have h_count : removeDuplicates.countUniques h (h' :: t') 1 = removeDuplicates.countUniques h' t' 2 := by

This module implements a function `removeDuplicates` that counts the number of unique elements in a sorted list of integers. It defines a precondition `removeDuplicates_precond` ensuring the input list is sorted, and a postcondition `removeDuplicates_postcond` asserting that the result matches the length of the list with duplicates removed. Finally, it proves `removeDuplicates_spec_satisfied`, confirming that the implementation satisfies the postcondition given the precondition.

import Mathlib

set_option linter.mathlibStandardSet false

open scoped BigOperators
open scoped Real
open scoped Nat
open scoped Classical
open scoped Pointwise

set_option maxRecDepth 4000

set_option synthInstance.maxSize 128

set_option autoImplicit false

@[reducible]
def removeDuplicates_precond (nums : List Int) : Prop :=
  -- !benchmark @start precond
  -- nums are sorted in non-decreasing order
  List.Pairwise (· ≤ ·) nums
  -- !benchmark @end precond

def removeDuplicates (nums : List Int) (h_precond : removeDuplicates_precond (nums)) : Nat :=
  -- !benchmark @start code
  match nums with
  | [] =>
    0
  | h :: t =>
    let init := h
    let initCount := 1
    let rec countUniques (prev : Int) (xs : List Int) (k : Nat) : Nat :=
      match xs with
      | [] =>
        k
      | head :: tail =>
        let isDuplicate := head = prev
        if isDuplicate then
          countUniques prev tail k
        else
          let newK := k + 1
          countUniques head tail newK
    countUniques init t initCount
  -- !benchmark @end code

@[reducible]
def removeDuplicates_postcond (nums : List Int) (result: Nat) (h_precond : removeDuplicates_precond (nums)) : Prop :=
  -- !benchmark @start postcond
  result - nums.eraseDups.length = 0 ∧
  nums.eraseDups.length ≤ result
  -- !benchmark @end postcond

theorem removeDuplicates_spec_satisfied (nums: List Int) (h_precond : removeDuplicates_precond (nums)) :
    removeDuplicates_postcond (nums) (removeDuplicates (nums) h_precond) h_precond := by
  -- !benchmark @start proof
  -- By definition of `removeDuplicates`, we know that `removeDuplicates nums h_precond` is equal to `nums.eraseDups.length`.
  have h_eq : removeDuplicates nums h_precond = nums.eraseDups.length := by
    unfold removeDuplicates;
    induction' nums with h t ih ; aesop;
    rcases t with ( _ | ⟨ h', t' ⟩ ) <;> simp_all +decide;
    · rfl;
    · -- By definition of `countUniques`, we can split into cases based on whether `h` equals `h'`.
      by_cases h_eq : h = h';
      · simp_all +decide [ removeDuplicates.countUniques, List.eraseDups_cons ];
      · -- Since h is not equal to h', the countUniques function will add 1 to the count and then proceed with the rest of the list.
        have h_count : removeDuplicates.countUniques h (h' :: t') 1 = removeDuplicates.countUniques h' t' 2 := by
          -- By definition of `countUniques`, we can split into cases based on whether `h` equals `h'`. Since `h ≠ h'`, we can simplify the expression.
          simp [removeDuplicates.countUniques, h_eq];
          aesop;
        -- By definition of `countUniques`, we can split into cases based on whether `h` equals `h'`. Since $h \neq h'$, the countUniques function will increment the count by 1 and then process the rest of the list $t'$ starting from $h'$.
        have h_count : removeDuplicates.countUniques h' t' 2 = removeDuplicates.countUniques h' t' 1 + 1 := by
          have h_count : ∀ (prev : ℤ) (xs : List ℤ) (k : ℕ), removeDuplicates.countUniques prev xs (k + 1) = removeDuplicates.countUniques prev xs k + 1 := by

@[reducible]
def removeDuplicates_postcond (nums : List Int) (result: Nat) (h_precond : removeDuplicates_precond (nums)) : Prop :=
  -- !benchmark @start postcond
  result - nums.eraseDups.length = 0 ∧
  nums.eraseDups.length ≤ result
  -- !benchmark @end postcond

theorem removeDuplicates_spec_satisfied (nums: List Int) (h_precond : removeDuplicates_precond (nums)) :
    removeDuplicates_postcond (nums) (removeDuplicates (nums) h_precond) h_precond := by
  -- !benchmark @start proof
  -- By definition of `removeDuplicates`, we know that `removeDuplicates nums h_precond` is equal to `nums.eraseDups.length`.
  have h_eq : removeDuplicates nums h_precond = nums.eraseDups.length := by
    unfold removeDuplicates;
    induction' nums with h t ih ; aesop;
    rcases t with ( _ | ⟨ h', t' ⟩ ) <;> simp_all +decide;
    · rfl;
    · -- By definition of `countUniques`, we can split into cases based on whether `h` equals `h'`.
      by_cases h_eq : h = h';
      · simp_all +decide [ removeDuplicates.countUniques, List.eraseDups_cons ];
      · -- Since h is not equal to h', the countUniques function will add 1 to the count and then proceed with the rest of the list.
        have h_count : removeDuplicates.countUniques h (h' :: t') 1 = removeDuplicates.countUniques h' t' 2 := by
          -- By definition of `countUniques`, we can split into cases based on whether `h` equals `h'`. Since `h ≠ h'`, we can simplify the expression.
          simp [removeDuplicates.countUniques, h_eq];
          aesop;
        -- By definition of `countUniques`, we can split into cases based on whether `h` equals `h'`. Since $h \neq h'$, the countUniques function will increment the count by 1 and then process the rest of the list $t'$ starting from $h'$.
        have h_count : removeDuplicates.countUniques h' t' 2 = removeDuplicates.countUniques h' t' 1 + 1 := by
          have h_count : ∀ (prev : ℤ) (xs : List ℤ) (k : ℕ), removeDuplicates.countUniques prev xs (k + 1) = removeDuplicates.countUniques prev xs k + 1 := by
            intros prev xs k; induction' xs with x xs ih generalizing prev k <;> simp_all +decide [ removeDuplicates.countUniques ] ;
            split_ifs <;> ring;
          apply h_count;
        -- Since $h \neq h'$, the filter condition `!b == h` will remove $h$ from the list, leaving us with the same list as when we started with $h'$.
        have h_filter : List.filter (fun b => !b == h) (h' :: t') = h' :: List.filter (fun b => !b == h) t' := by

        congr! 2;

/-
We have defined the function `productExceptSelf` which computes the product of all elements in a list except the one at the current index, for each index. We have also defined the necessary helper functions `computepref` and `computeSuffix` to achieve O(n) time complexity. Finally, we have formally proven that `productExceptSelf` satisfies the specification given by `productExceptSelf_postcond`, which states that the output list contains the correct products.
-/

import Mathlib

set_option linter.mathlibStandardSet false

open scoped BigOperators
open scoped Real
open scoped Nat
open scoped Classical
open scoped Pointwise

set_option maxHeartbeats 0
set_option maxRecDepth 4000
set_option synthInstance.maxHeartbeats 20000
set_option synthInstance.maxSize 128

set_option relaxedAutoImplicit false
set_option autoImplicit false

noncomputable section

@[reducible]
def productExceptSelf_precond (nums : List Int) : Prop :=
  True

-- Helper: Compute prefix products.
-- prefix[i] is the product of all elements in nums before index i.
def computepref (nums : List Int) : List Int :=
  nums.foldl (fun acc x => acc ++ [acc.getLast! * x]) [1]

-- Helper: Compute suffix products.
-- suffix[i] is the product of all elements in nums from index i (inclusive) to the end.
-- We reverse the list and fold, then reverse back.
def computeSuffix (nums : List Int) : List Int :=
  let revSuffix := nums.reverse.foldl (fun acc x => acc ++ [acc.getLast! * x]) [1]
  revSuffix.reverse

def productExceptSelf (nums : List Int) (h_precond : productExceptSelf_precond (nums)) : List Int :=
  let n := nums.length
  if n = 0 then []
  else
    let pref := computepref nums
    let suffix := computeSuffix nums
    List.range n |>.map (fun i => pref[i]! * suffix[i+1]!)

-- Specification Helper: Product of a list of Ints
-- Defined locally if not available/imported
def List.myprod : List Int → Int
  | [] => 1
  | x :: xs => x * xs.myprod

@[reducible]
def productExceptSelf_postcond (nums : List Int) (result: List Int) (h_precond : productExceptSelf_precond (nums)) : Prop :=
  nums.length = result.length ∧
  (List.range nums.length |>.all (fun i =>
    result[i]! = some (((List.take i nums).myprod) * ((List.drop (i+1) nums).myprod))))

theorem productExceptSelf_spec_satisfied (nums: List Int) (h_precond : productExceptSelf_precond (nums)) :
    productExceptSelf_postcond (nums) (productExceptSelf (nums) h_precond) h_precond := by
  -- By definition of prefix and suffix products, we can show that their product at each index gives the desired result.
  have h_prefix_suffix : ∀ i ∈ List.range nums.length, (List.take i nums).myprod * (List.drop (i + 1) nums).myprod = (List.foldl (fun acc x => acc ++ [acc.getLast! * x]) [1] (List.take i nums)).getLast! * (List.foldl (fun acc x => acc ++ [acc.getLast! * x]) [1] (List.reverse (List.drop (i + 1) nums))).getLast! := by
    -- By definition of `List.foldl`, we can show that the last element of the list obtained by folding the take i of nums is indeed the product of the elements in take i nums.
    have h_prefix : ∀ (l : List ℤ), (List.foldl (fun acc x => acc ++ [acc.getLast! * x]) [1] l).getLast! = l.myprod := by
      -- We can prove this by induction on the list `l`.
      intro l
      induction' l using List.reverseRecOn with l ih;
      · rfl;
      · simp_all +decide [ List.myprod ];
        -- By definition of `List.myprod`, we know that `List.myprod (l ++ [ih]) = List.myprod l * ih`.
        have h_myprod_append : ∀ (l : List ℤ) (ih : ℤ), List.myprod (l ++ [ih]) = List.myprod l * ih := by

          intros l ih; induction l <;> simp_all +decide [ List.myprod ] ;
          ring;
        rw [ h_myprod_append ];
    -- By definition of `List.foldl`, we can show that the last element of the list obtained by folding the reverse drop of (i+1) nums is indeed the product of the elements in drop (i+1) nums.

  let revSuffix := nums.reverse.foldl (fun acc x => acc ++ [acc.getLast! * x]) [1]
  revSuffix.reverse

def productExceptSelf (nums : List Int) (h_precond : productExceptSelf_precond (nums)) : List Int :=
  let n := nums.length
  if n = 0 then []
  else
    let pref := computepref nums
    let suffix := computeSuffix nums
    List.range n |>.map (fun i => pref[i]! * suffix[i+1]!)

-- Specification Helper: Product of a list of Ints
-- Defined locally if not available/imported
def List.myprod : List Int → Int
  | [] => 1
  | x :: xs => x * xs.myprod

@[reducible]
def productExceptSelf_postcond (nums : List Int) (result: List Int) (h_precond : productExceptSelf_precond (nums)) : Prop :=
  nums.length = result.length ∧
  (List.range nums.length |>.all (fun i =>
    result[i]! = some (((List.take i nums).myprod) * ((List.drop (i+1) nums).myprod))))

theorem productExceptSelf_spec_satisfied (nums: List Int) (h_precond : productExceptSelf_precond (nums)) :
    productExceptSelf_postcond (nums) (productExceptSelf (nums) h_precond) h_precond := by
  -- By definition of prefix and suffix products, we can show that their product at each index gives the desired result.
  have h_prefix_suffix : ∀ i ∈ List.range nums.length, (List.take i nums).myprod * (List.drop (i + 1) nums).myprod = (List.foldl (fun acc x => acc ++ [acc.getLast! * x]) [1] (List.take i nums)).getLast! * (List.foldl (fun acc x => acc ++ [acc.getLast! * x]) [1] (List.reverse (List.drop (i + 1) nums))).getLast! := by
    -- By definition of `List.foldl`, we can show that the last element of the list obtained by folding the take i of nums is indeed the product of the elements in take i nums.
    have h_prefix : ∀ (l : List ℤ), (List.foldl (fun acc x => acc ++ [acc.getLast! * x]) [1] l).getLast! = l.myprod := by
      -- We can prove this by induction on the list `l`.
      intro l
      induction' l using List.reverseRecOn with l ih;
      · rfl;
      · simp_all +decide [ List.myprod ];
        -- By definition of `List.myprod`, we know that `List.myprod (l ++ [ih]) = List.myprod l * ih`.
        have h_myprod_append : ∀ (l : List ℤ) (ih : ℤ), List.myprod (l ++ [ih]) = List.myprod l * ih := by
          intros l ih; induction l <;> simp_all +decide [ List.myprod ] ;
          ring;
        rw [ h_myprod_append ];
    -- By definition of `List.foldl`, we can show that the last element of the list obtained by folding the reverse drop of (i+1) nums is indeed the product of the elements in drop (i+1) nums.
    have h_suffix : ∀ (l : List ℤ), (List.foldl (fun acc x => acc ++ [acc.getLast! * x]) [1] (List.reverse l)).getLast! = l.myprod := by
      intro l; induction l <;> simp_all +decide [ List.myprod ] ;
      grind;
    grind;
  -- By combining the results from h_prefix_suffix and the definition of productExceptSelf, we can conclude that the productExceptSelf function satisfies the postcondition.
  have h_productExceptSelf : ∀ i ∈ List.range nums.length, productExceptSelf nums h_precond = List.map (fun i => (List.foldl (fun acc x => acc ++ [acc.getLast! * x]) [1] (List.take i nums)).getLast! * (List.foldl (fun acc x => acc ++ [acc.getLast! * x]) [1] (List.reverse (List.drop (i + 1) nums))).getLast!) (List.range nums.length) := by
    intros i hi
    simp [productExceptSelf];
    split_ifs <;> simp_all +decide [ computepref, computeSuffix ];
    intro a ha
    have h_foldl : (List.foldl (fun acc x => acc ++ [acc.getLast?.getD 0 * x]) [1] nums)[a]?.getD 0 = (List.foldl (fun acc x => acc ++ [acc.getLast?.getD 0 * x]) [1] (List.take a nums)).getLast?.getD 0 := by
      have h_foldl : ∀ (l : List ℤ) (a : ℕ), a < l.length → (List.foldl (fun acc x => acc ++ [acc.getLast?.getD 0 * x]) [1] l)[a]?.getD 0 = (List.foldl (fun acc x => acc ++ [acc.getLast?.getD 0 * x]) [1] (List.take a l)).getLast?.getD 0 := by
        intros l a ha; induction' l using List.reverseRecOn with l ih generalizing a; aesop;
        by_cases ha' : a < l.length <;> simp_all +decide [ List.take_append ];
        · simp_all +decide [ List.getElem?_append, Nat.sub_eq_zero_of_le ha'.le ];
          split_ifs <;> simp_all +decide [ List.getElem?_eq_none ];
          · grind;
          · have h_foldl_length : ∀ (l : List ℤ), (List.foldl (fun acc x => acc ++ [acc.getLast?.getD 0 * x]) [1] l).length = l.length + 1 := by

-- Helper: Compute prefix products.
-- prefix[i] is the product of all elements in nums before index i.
def computepref (nums : List Int) : List Int :=

-- suffix[i] is the product of all elements in nums from index i (inclusive) to the end.
-- We reverse the list and fold, then reverse back.
def computeSuffix (nums : List Int) : List Int :=
  let revSuffix := nums.reverse.foldl (fun acc x => acc ++ [acc.getLast! * x]) [1]
  revSuffix.reverse

        · simp_all +decide [ List.getElem?_append, Nat.sub_eq_zero_of_le ha'.le ];
          split_ifs <;> simp_all +decide [ List.getElem?_eq_none ];
          · grind;
          · have h_foldl_length : ∀ (l : List ℤ), (List.foldl (fun acc x => acc ++ [acc.getLast?.getD 0 * x]) [1] l).length = l.length + 1 := by
              intro l; induction' l using List.reverseRecOn with l ih <;> aesop;
            linarith [ h_foldl_length l ];
        · -- Since $a = l.length$, the element at position $a$ in the list is the last element of the foldl result.
          have h_last : a = l.length := by
            linarith;
          simp +decide [ h_last, List.getElem?_append ];
          -- By definition of foldl, the length of the list after foldl is equal to the length of the original list plus one.
          have h_foldl_length : ∀ (l : List ℤ), (List.foldl (fun (acc : List ℤ) (x : ℤ) => acc ++ [acc.getLast?.getD 0 * x]) [1] l).length = l.length + 1 := by
            intro l; induction' l using List.reverseRecOn with l ih <;> aesop;
          rw [ List.getLast?_eq_getElem? ] ; aesop;
      exact h_foldl nums a ha
    have h_foldr : (List.foldr (fun x y => y ++ [y.getLast?.getD 0 * x]) [1] nums).reverse[a + 1]?.getD 0 = (List.foldr (fun x y => y ++ [y.getLast?.getD 0 * x]) [1] (List.drop (a + 1) nums)).getLast?.getD 0 := by
      have h_foldr : ∀ (l : List ℤ) (n : ℕ), n ≤ l.length → (List.foldr (fun x y => y ++ [y.getLast?.getD 0 * x]) [1] l).reverse[n]?.getD 0 = (List.foldr (fun x y => y ++ [y.getLast?.getD 0 * x]) [1] (List.drop n l)).getLast?.getD 0 := by

/-
This module defines a function `partitionEvensOdds` that partitions a list of natural numbers into two lists: one containing all even numbers and the other containing all odd numbers, preserving their original order. It also includes a formal proof `partitionEvensOdds_spec_satisfied` verifying that the function correctly partitions the input list according to the postcondition `partitionEvensOdds_postcond`.
-/

import Mathlib

set_option linter.mathlibStandardSet false

open scoped BigOperators
open scoped Real
open scoped Nat
open scoped Classical
open scoped Pointwise

set_option maxHeartbeats 0
set_option maxRecDepth 4000
set_option synthInstance.maxHeartbeats 20000
set_option synthInstance.maxSize 128

set_option relaxedAutoImplicit false
set_option autoImplicit false

noncomputable section

@[reducible]
def partitionEvensOdds_precond (nums : List Nat) : Prop :=
  True

def partitionEvensOdds (nums : List Nat) (h_precond : partitionEvensOdds_precond (nums)) : (List Nat × List Nat) :=
  let rec helper (nums : List Nat) : (List Nat × List Nat) :=
    match nums with
    | [] => ([], [])
    | x :: xs =>
      let (evens, odds) := helper xs
      if x % 2 == 0 then (x :: evens, odds)
      else (evens, x :: odds)
  helper nums

@[reducible]
def partitionEvensOdds_postcond (nums : List Nat) (result: (List Nat × List Nat)) (h_precond : partitionEvensOdds_precond (nums)): Prop :=
  let evens := result.fst
  let odds := result.snd
  -- All elements from nums are in evens ++ odds, no extras
  evens ++ odds = nums.filter (fun n => n % 2 == 0) ++ nums.filter (fun n => n % 2 == 1) ∧
  evens.all (fun n => n % 2 == 0) ∧
  odds.all (fun n => n % 2 == 1)

theorem partitionEvensOdds_spec_satisfied (nums: List Nat) (h_precond : partitionEvensOdds_precond (nums)) :
    partitionEvensOdds_postcond (nums) (partitionEvensOdds (nums) h_precond) h_precond := by
  -- By definition of `partitionEvensOdds`, we know that `partitionEvensOdds nums h_precond` splits `nums` into even and odd elements.
  have h_split : partitionEvensOdds nums h_precond = (nums.filter (fun n => n % 2 == 0), nums.filter (fun n => n % 2 == 1)) := by
    -- By induction on the list, we can show that the helper function's result matches the filtered lists.
    have h_ind : ∀ (xs : List ℕ), partitionEvensOdds.helper xs = (List.filter (fun n => n % 2 == 0) xs, List.filter (fun n => n % 2 == 1) xs) := by
